# Regarding Stiltsmere

> **Use order for DMs:** read **A DM's Primer to Stiltsmere**, then **Sanity & Scaling Guide**, then **The Souring of Stiltsmere: A Campaign Guide**. The Layer documents and universal layer applications are support appendices, not required first-reading material.
>
> **Spoiler handling:** the opening player-facing material may be shared. Everything from **A DM's Primer to Stiltsmere** onward is DM-facing unless your table explicitly enjoys shared dramatic irony.

---

*"Don't stare too long at your own face in the Shine. It has a way of starin' back."*

---

There is a place on the Lake of Tears where the fog never lifts.

The locals call it the Shattering Shine — a permanent, mirror-still mist that blankets the water and creeps onto the shores of the three islands it surrounds. It reflects everything with perfect clarity. The boats, the birds, the faces of the people who live there. All of it returned to you in silver, exactly as it is.

Except for the mouths.

In the Shine, every reflection stands with its mouth open. Wide, and silent, and still. Not in pain. Not in warning. Simply open, the way a door is open, the way a wound is open. The islanders have a name for this too. They call it a Soursight, and they have learned not to look.

Stiltsmere is three islands lashed together by bridges over dark water. It is a cannery and a port town and a mountain community and a farming settlement built on stilts above flooded fields. It smells of fish and coal smoke and, underneath both, something faintly sweet and wrong. It is the kind of place that has been alone long enough to develop its own gravity — its own customs, its own silences, its own particular relationship with the thing that lives in the fog.

You are going there because someone died.

That is how it begins: a body in a cannery, a contract, a boat at dusk. The fisherman who takes you across the lake is old and does not make conversation. He sings instead, an old mournful tune about a mountain that broke and a valley that drowned and what was left when the water settled. You will remember the song later. You will remember it at inconvenient moments, when you are somewhere dark and the words come back to you whether you want them or not.

Stiltsmere will change you. This is not a threat or a promise — it is simply what the place does. The people who live there have been changed by it gradually, over generations, until the strangeness is so familiar they no longer notice it. You will notice it. And the more you notice, the more you will see. And the more you see, the more it will cost you.

There is a mechanic in this campaign called Sanity. It measures your character's connection to the world as they understood it before they came here. It goes down. This is expected. This is, in a sense, the point. But Sanity is not simply a second health bar counting toward a bad ending. It is a key. The less of it you have, the more of Stiltsmere becomes visible to you — its hidden paths, its true inhabitants, its older and deeper architecture. The world does not get worse as your Sanity decreases. It gets *larger*. It gets *realer*. Whether that is better or worse is a question the campaign will ask you and decline to answer on your behalf.

You will not be heroes in the traditional sense. You will be investigators, and then witnesses, and then — if you are lucky and careful and occasionally brave at the right moments — you will be the people who stopped something that had been building for ten thousand years. Or you will be the people who almost did. Or you will be the people who watched it complete itself and survived to carry that knowledge somewhere else.

All of these are valid outcomes. All of them are true to what Stiltsmere is.

The boat leaves at dusk. Silas will not wait.

---

## What Kind of Game Is This?

Stiltsmere is a **cosmic horror mystery campaign** for 3–5 players using the D&D 5th Edition ruleset, designed to run across multiple sessions and character levels. It begins as a murder investigation and becomes something considerably larger.

The campaign prioritises **investigation, atmosphere, and meaningful choice** over combat as the primary mode of engagement. Fights happen — some of them are brutal — but they are rarely the solution to a problem. More often they are the consequence of a problem that wasn't solved in time.

The tone is one of **creeping dread and earned revelation**. Horror here is not sudden. It is gradual, cumulative, and deeply tied to the mechanics of the game itself. The rules are designed to show you what is happening rather than tell you, and the world is designed to reward curiosity while ensuring that curiosity has a cost.

**This campaign is particularly well suited for groups who:**
- Enjoy roleplay and investigation as much as or more than combat
- Are comfortable with ambiguous outcomes and morally complex situations
- Want a game that takes their characters' inner lives seriously
- Are willing to engage with a custom mechanical system that will feel unfamiliar at first and intuitive by the third session

**This campaign may not be the right fit for groups who:**
- Prefer clear heroic objectives and unambiguous victory conditions
- Find resource management under narrative pressure frustrating rather than exciting
- Want to engage primarily through combat encounters
- Are not comfortable with body horror, psychological distress, or the possibility of permanent character consequences

---

## The Three Islands

**The Isle of Murk** is where you begin. The port town of Stiltsmere sits here — the cannery, the inn, the harbormaster's office, the wealthiest family's estate on the hill. It is the most ordinary of the three islands, which is not saying much. The fog is thickest here at night. The people are the most closed.

**The Isle of Crags** is south, connected by a high stone bridge. The mountains here are sharp enough to cut the fog into strange shapes, and the wind through the rocks has always sounded like voices. The people who live here, the Crag-herders, have decided the voices are the mountain speaking. They may be right. They may be right in ways they don't fully understand yet.

**The Isle of Reeds** is north, connected by a long wooden causeway that sways in the lake wind. The ground here is mostly water — flooded fields where bioluminescent plants grow beneath the surface and the farmers walk on stilts above the fog. The people here are the most reclusive. They have reasons.

Beneath all three islands, beneath the lake itself, is a fourth location that does not appear on any map and cannot be reached by any ordinary means. Its name is The City in Mercury. It has been there for ten thousand years. It is the reason for all of this.

---

## A Note on What You Will Experience

This campaign was developed over multiple iterations and tested with several independent groups, each of whom had a substantially different experience of it. The story responds to what you do. The ending you reach will be the ending your choices built.

Some of what you encounter will be beautiful. The Glimmer Fields at night, the view from the lighthouse peak, a moment of unexpected human connection in an inn that smells of smoke and salt. The campaign is designed to earn its horror through contrast — the beautiful moments matter because they are real, and they are real because they are at risk.

Some of what you encounter will be deeply unsettling. The campaign does not apologise for this, but it does ask that your table discuss comfort levels and safety tools before the first session. A brief conversation at the start costs nothing and protects everyone's experience.

The fisherman's song has four verses. By the time you understand all of them, the campaign will be nearly over.

*Welcome to Stiltsmere.*

---

*"The Souring of Stiltsmere" is a free campaign for D&D 5th Edition. The full campaign guide, DM primer, and complete bestiary are available alongside this document.*

---

# The Economics of Layered DMing
### Why the Layer System Costs Less Than You Think

---

## Read This First

If you looked at the Layer System described in "About Layers" and thought *that sounds like a lot of work*, this document is for you.

That reaction is understandable and it is also wrong, and the gap between the perception and the reality is worth closing before it stops you from using one of the most effective tools in this campaign. The Layer System sounds complex because it has been described in detail. Detailed descriptions of simple things can make them sound complicated. A detailed description of how to tie a shoelace sounds alarming. Tying a shoelace takes four seconds.

Here is the economic reality of the Layer System stated as plainly as possible:

**You are not replacing your maps. You are adding small objects to them.**

That is the entire correction. Everything else in this document explains why that correction matters and how to apply it practically.

---

## The Misreading and Why It Happens

The most common misreading of the Layer System is this: a DM reads "three layers per location" and mentally calculates three full maps per location, multiplied by every location in the campaign, and arrives at a number that sounds like a full-time job.

This misreading is natural because most visual map systems in tabletop gaming are replacement systems — you have the dungeon map for Room A, and when the party reveals a hidden chamber you swap it for Room A Plus Hidden Chamber. The assumption carries over.

The Layer System is not a replacement system. It is an addition system.

Your base map never changes. What changes is what sits on top of it. A Layer 1 ghost figure is a piece of transparent acetate roughly the size of a miniature, placed on the map when a character crosses the threshold, removed when they don't need to see it anymore. A hidden doorway is a door-shaped cutout placed against a wall section. A bloodstain is an irregular shape placed on the floor.

None of these require a new map. None of them require significant preparation once your initial library is built. All of them take approximately three seconds to place or remove.

---

## The Real Preparation Arithmetic

Here is what the Layer System actually costs in time and money.

### Money

The materials for a complete physical layer library for the entire campaign:

- One pack of acetate transparency sheets: approximately $8–12
- Dry-erase or permanent markers in three colours: approximately $4–6
- Scissors: you already own scissors
- A small accordion folder or box for storage: approximately $5–8

**Total: $17–26 for the entire campaign.**

This is less than a single set of dice. It is significantly less than most published adventure supplements. If you already own transparency film and markers, the cost is zero.

For digital VTT implementations, the cost is also zero — every major platform has native layer functionality built in.

### Time

The time investment breaks into two categories: the initial library-building session and per-session preparation.

**The initial library-building session** occurs once before the campaign begins. You sit down with your transparency sheets and markers and create every universal element the campaign will ever use — the ghost figures, the footprint trails, the sigils, the doorway shapes. This takes approximately two to three hours depending on how detailed you make your elements.

After this session, you are done building. You are not building again.

**Per-session preparation** for the layer system consists of: looking at the location you are running, identifying which layer elements belong there, pulling them from your storage folder, and noting on an index card which pieces go where. For a standard session with two or three major locations, this adds approximately ten to fifteen minutes to your normal session prep.

**Total ongoing time investment: ten to fifteen minutes per session.**

Compare this to the hours a DM might spend drawing maps, preparing encounter tables, or writing NPC dialogue. The Layer System is one of the least time-intensive components of running this campaign.

### The Three-Hour Preparation Standard

The Souring of Stiltsmere is designed to be fully runnable within a three-hour preparation window per session. The Layer System is not the obstacle to that standard — it is one of the tools that makes it achievable, because physical elements placed on a map communicate information that would otherwise require pages of description or complex player-facing handouts.

A ghost figure placed on the map in three seconds tells a player something that takes two minutes to describe in words. A hidden doorway cutout placed against a wall tells a player "there is something here your character can perceive" without any verbal communication from the DM at all. Physical elements do cognitive work that saves time rather than consuming it.

---

## The Cost Comparison

To make the economics concrete, here is a side-by-side comparison of what the Layer System costs versus what it might feel like it costs.

| What It Feels Like | What It Actually Is |
|---|---|
| Three full alternate maps per location | Small cutouts placed on one base map |
| Hours of artistic preparation | Two to three hours once, before campaign begins |
| Expensive specialist materials | Acetate sheets, markers, scissors |
| Complex tracking system | Pull pieces from a folder, place on map, remove when done |
| A different map for each Understanding Tier | The same map with different small objects on it |
| Rebuilding your entire prep when a player loses Sanity | Placing or removing a cutout in three seconds |

The Layer System looks expensive because it is described in detail. It costs what a coffee costs and takes what a lunch break takes, once.

---

## The Minimum Viable Layer System

For DMs who want to start with the smallest possible investment and scale up, here is the minimum viable implementation that still delivers the system's core value.

**Build only five elements before your first session:**

1. Four standing ghost figures (Layer 1, silver marker on acetate)
2. Two footprint trail strips (Layer 1, silver marker)
3. Two hidden door shapes (Layer 2, blue-white marker)
4. Two glowing sigils (Layer 1, silver marker)
5. One grotto entrance for the Salty Squid (Layer 2, blue-white marker)

This is twenty to thirty minutes of work and costs approximately $8 in materials. These five element types cover the majority of what the first four sessions require.

After your first session, you will understand exactly how the system works at your table. Add elements session by session based on what you actually need rather than building everything in advance. The full library checklist in "The DM's Layer Library" document is the comprehensive version — you do not need it all at once.

---

## What You Are Actually Doing at the Table

During a session, the Layer System looks like this:

A player's character drops to 49 Sanity. You reach into your folder, pull out a ghost figure and a footprint trail, and place them on the Salty Squid map. This takes four seconds. You continue narrating without pause.

Later, a Whispering Mender heals that character back above 50 Sanity. You pick up the ghost figure and the footprint trail. This takes two seconds.

That is the entirety of the layer mechanic in physical execution. Everything else — the psychological impact on players, the dramatic irony of some players seeing things others cannot, the moment when a character's sanity shifts mid-combat and something appears on the map — that emerges from the two-second act of placing or removing a small piece of transparent plastic.

The system is not complex to run. It looks complex to read about because written descriptions of spatial mechanics always look more demanding than the spatial mechanics themselves. Once you have done it at a table once, it will feel as natural as placing a miniature.

---

## The Summary Argument

The Layer System costs:
- One preparation session of two to three hours
- Ten to fifteen minutes of layer-specific prep per session
- Approximately $20 in total materials

The Layer System delivers:
- Visual communication of mechanical information without verbal explanation
- A physical teaching mechanism that shows players how Sanity loss changes perception
- Dramatic tension from shared out-of-character awareness and in-character limitation
- A reusable prop library that grows more useful the longer the campaign runs

The return on this investment is not proportional. You are spending very little to receive one of the most effective table communication tools in the campaign.

The Layer System appears harder than it is. Once you understand that you are adding small objects to existing maps rather than replacing those maps, the apparent difficulty dissolves entirely.

Build your library once. Run it forever.

---

*"The Economics of Layered DMing" is a companion document to "About Layers" and "The DM's Layer Library" for "The Souring of Stiltsmere: A Campaign Guide."*

---

# About Layers
### A Complete Guide to the Layered Map System for "The Souring of Stiltsmere"

---

## What This Document Is

This is the practical implementation guide for the campaign's central visual mechanic: the Layered Map System. If you have read the main campaign guide and felt uncertain about the amount of preparation the layer system requires, this document will resolve that uncertainty. The system is significantly more flexible and less labour-intensive than it may appear from description alone.

Read this document before you build anything. The approach it describes will save you hours of preparation time and produce better results at the table than any alternative.

---

## The Core Misunderstanding to Correct First

The layered map system does **not** require you to prepare a complete alternate version of every map for every Understanding Tier.

This is the most common misreading of the system and it is understandable — the document describes layers as "translucent overlays," which implies full-sheet coverage. In practice, the system works through **modular cutouts**: small, individual, transparent elements that represent specific layer-visible objects, creatures, clues, and phenomena. These cutouts are placed on the base map when the relevant Understanding Tier is active, and removed when it is not.

A ghost. A set of wet footprints. A glowing sigil. A bloodstain that fades in and out. A doorway where a wall used to be. A creature standing in a corner.

Each of these is a separate cutout, roughly the size of the object it represents. Each is reusable across any map in the campaign where that element appears. You build the cutout once. You use it everywhere.

This means the total preparation investment for the entire layer system across the whole campaign is a **single library-building session** before the campaign begins, plus minor additions as new specific elements arise. After that, running layers at the table is a matter of placing and removing pieces from a box — faster than tracking initiative.

---

## The Two Types of Layer Elements

Everything in the layer system falls into one of two categories.

### Type 1: Universal Elements
These are layer-visible phenomena that appear across multiple locations throughout the campaign. Because they recur, you build them once and reuse them indefinitely.

*Examples:*
- Ghostly figures (standing, seated, walking, pointing)
- Wet footprints (sets of 3–4 footprints in a trail)
- Glowing sigils (the cult's spiral symbol, The City in Mercury script)
- Soursight reflections (distorted face shapes, open-mouth imagery)
- Bloodstains (fresh, faded, arranged in patterns)
- Spectral handprints
- Organic conduit traces (for the Layer 2 bioluminescent network in the Whispering Caves)
- Void patches (areas of absolute non-reflection)
- Shadow anomalies (shadows falling the wrong direction)

These are your core library. Most of your preparation time goes here.

### Type 2: Location-Specific Elements
These are layer-visible phenomena that only appear in one place: a specific hidden door, a particular creature stationed at a particular threshold, the grotto entrance in the Salty Squid's corner wall, the Petrified Heart's beating glow.

Location-specific elements are prepared as needed, usually as part of normal session prep for the specific location. They are typically small and simple — a door-shaped cutout, a creature token at reduced opacity, a glowing circle.

---

## Physical Implementation: Three Methods

Choose the method that fits your table setup. All three work; the right choice depends on whether you play in person or digitally, and how much physical crafting you enjoy.

### Method 1: Acetate Cutouts (Physical Tables, Recommended)

**Materials:** Transparent acetate sheets (available at any office supply store), dry-erase markers or permanent markers in multiple colours, scissors or a craft knife, a small storage box or folder.

**Process:**
1. Cut acetate into pieces roughly sized to the element you are creating. A ghost figure needs a piece about 2–3 inches tall. A set of footprints needs a strip about 4 inches long. A door-shaped opening needs a rectangle matching the scale of your map.
2. Draw or trace the element onto the acetate in the appropriate colour. Use the colour coding system described below.
3. Label the back of each piece lightly in dry-erase marker with its tier number (1, 2, or 3) so you can find what you need quickly.
4. Store all pieces in a small box or accordion folder, sorted by tier.

**At the table:** When a character crosses a tier threshold, open the relevant section of your box and place the appropriate pieces on the map as described below. When Sanity is restored past a threshold, remove those pieces.

**Cost:** Approximately 5–10 dollars in materials for the entire campaign.

### Method 2: Printed Transparencies (Physical Tables, Higher Fidelity)

**Materials:** Transparency film compatible with your printer, a printer, image editing software (even free tools like Canva or GIMP work).

**Process:**
1. Create digital versions of your layer elements — ghostly figures, sigils, footprints — at low opacity (30–50% is usually right).
2. Print them onto transparency film.
3. Cut out individual elements and store as above.

This method produces more polished-looking results and is worth the additional effort if you have the tools. Printed transparencies at 40% opacity placed over a physical battlemap create exactly the visual effect the system is designed to produce.

### Method 3: Digital VTT Layers (Virtual Tables)

Every major virtual tabletop platform (Foundry, Roll20, Fantasy Grounds, Owlbear Rodeo) has a native layer system that maps directly onto this campaign's mechanic.

**Implementation:**
- Create a separate layer for each Understanding Tier (Layer 1, Layer 2, Layer 3) above the base map layer.
- Place all layer-specific elements on their corresponding digital layer.
- When a character crosses a tier threshold, make that layer visible to the relevant player(s). All players can see it at the DM level — toggle player visibility based on which characters have reached the tier.
- Use the platform's opacity controls to achieve the translucent visual effect.

**Note on partial visibility:** Most VTT platforms allow per-player layer visibility, which means you can show Layer 1 content only to players whose characters have reached Tier 1. This is mechanically correct but loses some of the table's dramatic irony — the out-of-character awareness that creates tension when a Tier 0 player watches their companion fight something they cannot see. Consider making layer content visible to all players out-of-character, and trusting the group to maintain the in-character distinction. Most groups handle this naturally and prefer the shared dramatic tension.

---

## The Colour Coding System

When creating layer elements, use consistent colours to communicate tier at a glance — both for your own reference when pulling pieces from your box and as a subtle visual signal to players about what they are perceiving.

| Tier | Recommended Colour | Visual Character |
|---|---|---|
| Layer 1 — The Crack | Silver/pale grey, semi-transparent | Faint, flickering, almost mistaken for the light |
| Layer 2 — The Glimpse | Cool blue-white, more opaque | Clearly present, clearly wrong |
| Layer 3 — The Knowing | Deep blue-green, nearly solid | Undeniable, overwhelming, real |

This is a recommendation, not a rule. If your table responds better to a different system — warm versus cool colours, or monochrome versus colour — adapt accordingly. The goal is consistency so players develop an instinct for what they are looking at.

---

## Building Your Layer Library: A Session Zero Checklist

The following checklist is the **minimum starter library**: enough to run the first several sessions cleanly without overwhelming prep. The fuller quantities appear later in **The DM's Layer Library** and should be treated as the campaign-complete build list. If the two lists ever appear to differ, this section is intentionally the starter version and the later library is the full version.

### Layer 1 Elements (Silver/Pale Grey)

- [ ] **Ghost figures x4** — standing, featureless humanoid outlines at roughly miniature scale. These are your most-used pieces. Make more than you think you need.
- [ ] **Seated ghost figure x2** — for the Salty Squid's spectral patrons and similar scenes.
- [ ] **Pointing ghost figure x1** — the spectral fisherman in the Salty Squid who points at the painting.
- [ ] **Wet footprint trail x3** — sets of 4–5 footprints in a line, roughly to scale with your map grid. Vary the size slightly.
- [ ] **Glowing spiral sigil x4** — the cult's symbol, small enough to fit in a wall section or floor tile.
- [ ] **Faint bloodstain x2** — irregular organic shapes.
- [ ] **Whisper lines x2** — faint curved lines emanating from a point, suggesting sound or presence.
- [ ] **Eye shape x2** — a simple open eye, used for Watcher phenomena and similar.

### Layer 2 Elements (Cool Blue-White)

- [ ] **Solid ghost figures x4** — same shapes as Layer 1 but more opaque, more present.
- [ ] **Hidden door/archway x3** — rectangle or arch shapes at map scale. These are your most structurally important Layer 2 pieces. A hidden door that appears in a wall changes the physical geography of the encounter.
- [ ] **Hidden staircase x2** — a simple staircase shape.
- [ ] **Spectral creature tokens** — at-opacity versions of Layer 2 creatures (Reflected Stalkers, Tangled Thought-Forms, Whispering Menders). These can simply be regular creature tokens printed at reduced opacity on transparency film.
- [ ] **Organic conduit traces x4** — branching vein-like lines for the Whispering Caves' bioluminescent network.
- [ ] **Hidden chasm x1** — a dark irregular shape indicating a drop that isn't visible at Layer 0.
- [ ] **Ritual circle x2** — concentric circle pattern at various scales.
- [ ] **Soursight face x3** — distorted face shapes with open mouths, for ambient layer-visible horror.

### Layer 3 Elements (Deep Blue-Green)

- [ ] **Fossilised anatomy indicators x3** — rib curve shapes, large organic forms for the Whispering Caves' petrified creature revelation.
- [ ] **Energy conduit network x1** — a larger version of the organic conduit traces showing the full network converging.
- [ ] **Cosmic string lines x4** — thin lines connecting points across a map, representing the "strings of reality" visible at Tier 3.
- [ ] **Deep shaft indicator x1** — a dark circle with depth implied, for the void-circle in the Whispering Caves' ritual chamber.
- [ ] **Reality fracture lines x3** — jagged crack shapes for areas of extreme Shine influence.

---

## Placing Elements at the Table: Practical Guidance

### When to place elements

Place a new tier's elements the moment a character crosses the threshold — not at the end of the scene, not at a convenient break. The immediacy of the placement is part of the mechanic's impact. A character drops to 49 Sanity in the middle of an encounter and you immediately place the Layer 2 pieces on the map. They now see things that weren't there thirty seconds ago, in the middle of combat, with no pause for adjustment. That is the experience the system is designed to create.

### Where to place elements

Elements go where the narrative logic of the location demands. A ghostly figure goes where a ghost would stand — near the hearth, in the corner where someone died, at the threshold of the room. Wet footprints go in a direction that implies a path. Sigils go on walls where the cult would have placed them. Use your established knowledge of the location to place elements that feel like discoveries rather than decorations.

### How many elements to place at once

Restraint produces better results than abundance, especially at Tier 1. One or two elements at a threshold crossing — a single ghost, a single trail of footprints — is more unsettling than five. The player's imagination will populate the space beyond what you show them. Trust that tendency. Add more elements over subsequent scenes as the tier becomes established.

### When to remove elements

When a character's Sanity is restored past a tier threshold — whether by a Whispering Mender, a Glimmer Bloom Vial, or any other restoration method — remove the corresponding tier's elements from their field of view. Do not make an announcement. Simply remove the pieces as the restoration takes effect. Let the player notice that what was there is no longer there. The realisation that healing has taken something away is more powerful than any explanation.

---

## The Modular Advantage: Composing Scenes From Your Library

Once your library is built, encounter preparation changes character. Instead of designing a layer layout for each location, you are **composing** a layer presence from available pieces.

A typical composition process for a new location takes approximately five minutes:

1. Look at the base map. Identify 2–3 points where layer-visible content would feel narratively appropriate.
2. Pull the relevant pieces from your box.
3. Note (on a small index card) which pieces go where, and at which tier they become visible.
4. Done.

The same ghost figure you placed in the Salty Squid appears in the Congregation Chamber three sessions later. The same footprint trail you used to lead players to the hidden alcove in the Dripping Gallery appears again pointing toward the concealed door in the Blackwood Estate study. Players may not consciously register that the pieces are the same — the context is entirely different — but there is an unconscious consistency to the visual language that makes the world feel coherent.

Over a full campaign, your library accumulates meaning. Players develop a conditioned response to seeing silver-tinted shapes on the map. The colour alone begins to communicate threat. This is a form of visual storytelling that rulebooks do not create and cannot be faked — it emerges from consistent use of the same physical vocabulary across many sessions.

---

## Using Layers for New Pathways: The Most Powerful Application

The layer system's most transformative use is not adding enemies or clues to a known space. It is **adding geography** — making a wall into a door, a dead end into a passage, a ruin into a navigable building.

This application is underused by DMs who think of layers primarily as a vision effect. It should be thought of as the primary application.

**How it works physically:** A door-shaped or archway-shaped cutout placed against a wall section on the base map indicates that characters at the relevant tier can perceive and use an opening that does not exist for lower-tier characters. The wall is still physically there for Tier 0 characters — they walk into it if they try to pass through. The Tier 2 character ahead of them has simply stepped through a doorway that their companions cannot see.

This creates some of the campaign's most memorable physical comedy and emotional tension simultaneously. A Tier 2 character who has found the entrance to the Weeping Fresco stands at a wall their Tier 0 companions see as solid, and says "it's right here, just — come through." And their companions cannot. The mechanic has generated a scene that no scripted encounter could.

**For the Whispering Caves specifically:** The descent to Area 10 (the Petrified Heart) is marked as requiring Understanding Tier 3 to fully access — not because the physical passage is blocked, but because at lower tiers it reads as a dead-end alcove. The carved stone door is visible. The mechanism to open it is not, until Tier 3. A door-shaped cutout with a small lock-mechanism detail appears at the Tier 3 threshold and the passage becomes navigable. Characters below Tier 3 who enter simply find a small, unremarkable stone room with a strange vibration in the floor.

**For The City in Mercury:** The entire Tesseract Labyrinth is a geography puzzle built on this principle. Archway cutouts placed in different wall sections of an otherwise identical-looking cubic room indicate which exits are currently aligned with three-dimensional space. As the party presses different symbols, you move the cutouts. The room has not changed. The accessible geometry has.

---

## A Note on Trust

The layer system asks something of your players that most mechanics do not: the ability to hold simultaneous in-character and out-of-character awareness without breaking immersion. All players can see the layer elements on the map regardless of their character's Understanding Tier. They are expected to act as though they cannot.

Most groups handle this more naturally than DMs expect. The separation is not difficult to maintain when the stakes feel real and the fiction is engaging. Players who know their character cannot see the ghost standing behind their companion are not less frightened by the ghost — they are more frightened, because they are experiencing both the helplessness of not knowing and the dramatic irony of watching something their character cannot perceive.

If a group struggles with the separation, establish a simple hand signal or table convention: when a player touches the layer element, they are engaging with it in-character. When they point to it without touching, they are noting it out-of-character. This distinction resolves most ambiguity without interrupting the scene.

Trust your players. The system rewards the trust.

---

*"About Layers" is a companion document to "The Souring of Stiltsmere: A Campaign Guide." It should be read before the first session of preparation.*

---

# Universal Layer Applications
### Using the Layer System in Any Campaign

---

## What This Document Is

The Layer System was developed for "The Souring of Stiltsmere," but the tools it uses — small, transparent cutouts placed on base maps to represent things that are hidden, supernatural, or only partially perceived — are not specific to that campaign. They are a general DM toolkit for any game where the visible world and the full world are not the same thing.

That description applies to almost every tabletop campaign ever run.

This document explains how to take the physical library you have built for Stiltsmere and use it in any game you run, and how to think about building new layer elements for campaigns that have nothing to do with cosmic horror or Sanity mechanics. By the end, you will have a permanent prop toolkit that travels with you from campaign to campaign and gets more useful the longer you use it.

---

## The Core Principle, Restated for Any Game

In Stiltsmere, layer elements represent things that characters can only perceive when their Understanding is high enough. But the underlying principle — *place a physical object on the map to represent something that exists but is not immediately visible* — applies anywhere you want to communicate hidden information visually.

Consider how often this situation arises in a standard campaign:

- A character with high Perception notices something others don't
- A rogue has Darkvision and can see the guard in the shadows
- A wizard detects a magical aura on an object in the room
- A ranger tracks footprints that an untrained eye wouldn't notice
- A paladin senses an aura of evil behind a friendly-looking face
- A cleric receives a vision of what this room looked like before a catastrophe
- A character is poisoned or cursed and perceives things incorrectly

Every one of these situations is a layer mechanic. One character perceives something that others don't, or perceives something incorrectly. The layer system gives you a physical way to represent that difference at the table — to show rather than tell, and to create the shared out-of-character awareness and in-character limitation that makes those moments dramatically interesting rather than just mechanically noted.

---

## Universal Element Types and Their Applications

The following element types from the Stiltsmere library work in almost any campaign with minimal or no modification. Each entry describes what the element communicates and gives examples across multiple genres and game systems.

---

### Ghost Figures
*Translucent humanoid silhouettes at miniature scale*

**In Stiltsmere:** Spectral patrons visible to characters with Understanding Tier 1 or higher.

**In Any Campaign:**
- **Standard haunted location:** A ghost visible only to the character who has been given the Sight, or who rolled highest on a Perception check in a supernatural location. Place the figure when that character is looking in the right direction. Remove it when they look away or the ghost moves.
- **Death retrospective:** A character with a special ability or item that allows them to see echoes of past events. The ghost figure represents a person who died in this room, going about their last moments. Other characters see nothing.
- **Divine vision:** A cleric or paladin receives a momentary vision of a celestial presence in the room — an angel, a divine messenger, a manifestation of their deity. The figure appears for one round and vanishes.
- **Villain's tell:** A shapeshifter or disguised enemy has a spectral echo of their true form visible only to the character who has True Seeing active. Place a ghost figure over or beside the NPC's token. That player knows. Others don't.
- **Horror encounter:** A character who has been isolated or is suffering from a curse begins seeing figures in empty spaces. Place ghost figures in corners and doorways. The DM knows which are real encounters and which are hallucinations. The player doesn't.

---

### Footprint Trails
*Sequential footprint strips indicating direction of travel*

**In Stiltsmere:** Wet footprints visible to Tier 1 characters, leading toward hidden locations.

**In Any Campaign:**
- **Investigation scenes:** The ranger or the character with Survival proficiency sees tracks that others miss. Place the strip for that player only; other players are told their characters see nothing. The trail leads toward a relevant clue.
- **Invisible enemy:** A creature using Greater Invisibility or similar is moving through the combat space. Place a footprint strip at the end of each of its turns to show where it moved. Characters with See Invisibility see the creature token; others see only the prints.
- **Environmental storytelling:** Before the party arrives, something passed through. Placing a faded or partially translucent print trail communicates that someone else was here recently without requiring the DM to narrate exposition.
- **Chase sequences:** A fleeing NPC leaves a visible trail for the tracker in the party and no visible trail for everyone else. The party must decide whether to follow the tracker's lead or split up.
- **Curse or haunt:** A location where something terrible happened still shows the footprints of the dead walking their last path. Only characters with the relevant ability or above a certain Perception DC can see them.

---

### Hidden Door / Archway Shapes
*Door or archway outlines placed against wall sections*

**In Stiltsmere:** The hidden entrance to the Weeping Fresco, visible only to characters at Understanding Tier 2.

**In Any Campaign:**
- **Secret passages:** The rogue's Passive Perception is high enough to spot a seam in the wall. Place the door shape. Other characters need to actively search and roll. The rogue already knows it's there.
- **Magical concealment:** A door hidden by an *Arcane Lock* and illusion spell is invisible to everyone without *See Invisibility* or *Dispel Magic*. The wizard who casts *Detect Magic* sees the outline — place the cutout for them.
- **Dimensional spaces:** An entrance to a pocket dimension, a fey crossing, or an extraplanar location that only manifests under specific conditions (a full moon, at midnight, when a specific object is present). The door shape appears when the condition is met.
- **Faction-specific knowledge:** Only members of a specific faction, thieves' guild, or religion know this door exists. Characters with the relevant background see it; others don't. Dramatically interesting when the party contains both.
- **Perception threshold rewards:** Any time you want to reward a high Perception or Investigation roll with more than just verbal confirmation — "yes, you find the secret door" — placing a physical shape is significantly more satisfying for players than a verbal description. It makes the discovery feel real.

---

### Glowing Sigils
*Small symbolic shapes that indicate magical or supernatural marking*

**In Stiltsmere:** The cult's spiral symbol, visible in its glowing form only to Tier 1+ characters.

**In Any Campaign:**
- **Detect Magic:** A character using this spell or ability sees the aura of magical items and enchantments. Place a sigil on or near any object that is magically significant. Scale the opacity to the strength of the aura.
- **Trap identification:** The rogue with the highest Investigation spots the pressure-plate rune or the trigger sigil for a magical trap. Place the sigil; other characters need to make their own checks to see it.
- **Territorial markers:** Factions, guilds, cults, or creatures mark their territory with symbols that only members or those who know to look for them can read. A character with the relevant knowledge sees the mark; others walk past it.
- **Divine or infernal marking:** A character who has been touched by a god, a demon lord, or a similar entity carries an invisible mark visible only to beings of the same alignment or type. An NPC who can see the mark reacts to it; the player may not yet know the mark is visible.
- **Historical layering:** The ruins the party is exploring were once something else. A character who makes a high History check or uses a specific ability sees the original marking beneath the centuries of wear — what this place was called, what it was used for, who built it.

---

### Spectral Creature Tokens
*Standard creature tokens at reduced opacity*

**In Stiltsmere:** Reflected Stalkers and other layer-visible creatures, visible only to characters at the appropriate Understanding Tier.

**In Any Campaign:**
- **Invisible enemies:** Any creature using invisibility, ethereal movement, or similar abilities. The token at 40% opacity tells the whole table out-of-character that something is there while preserving in-character uncertainty for characters without See Invisibility.
- **The ethereal plane:** Characters who can see into or move through the Ethereal Plane see entities there that others cannot. Place ethereal creature tokens for the relevant player.
- **Illusions:** A powerful illusion creates an apparent creature. Some characters see through it (ghost token — not quite real), others do not (full token). The DM knows which is which; the players must figure it out.
- **Death and dying:** A character at 0 HP is partially pulled toward the beyond. For one round before stabilising, they see creatures in the space that others cannot — echoes of the recently dead, psychopomps, or whatever your cosmology provides.
- **Prophetic vision:** A character with the Oracle background, a specific feat, or a class feature occasionally sees creatures that haven't arrived yet — a flash of what is coming. Place the ghost token. The player knows something is coming. They don't know when.

---

### Shadow Anomalies
*Shadows that don't match any physical object in the scene*

**In Stiltsmere:** Subtle Layer 1 presence indicators — something is here that shouldn't be.

**In Any Campaign:**
- **Possession indicator:** An NPC who is secretly possessed, influenced, or inhabited by something else casts a subtly wrong shadow visible to the character with the highest Insight or Perception. The shadow is the first tell.
- **Horror atmosphere:** In any horror-adjacent game, shadow anomalies placed in empty areas of the map create ambient dread without requiring an encounter. Something is wrong here. The shadows know.
- **Divination echo:** A location where powerful magic was once used retains a shadow of its own past. The shadow anomaly marks where the magic happened — not a creature, not a trap, but a place worth investigating.
- **Environmental storytelling:** The shadow of a hanged man still falls from the beam in the room even though no body is there. Only characters who have been told the history of the building, or who succeed on a History check, understand what they're seeing.

---

### Organic Conduit Traces / Thread Lines
*Fine connecting lines suggesting something runs between points*

**In Stiltsmere:** The bioluminescent network visible in the Whispering Caves at Layer 2.

**In Any Campaign:**
- **Detect Magic auras:** Lines of force connecting enchanted items to their source or to each other. A character who casts *Detect Magic* sees the connections between the seven ritual stones, understanding they function as a network rather than as individual objects.
- **Ley lines:** In systems where geographical magic flows along specific paths, a character with the relevant ability can see the lines passing through the current location. The map becomes a diagram of power.
- **Binding magic:** A creature or person bound by a geas, a curse, or a magical contract has a visible thread connecting them to the thing that binds them. The character who can see it understands something about the NPC's constraints that isn't apparent from conversation.
- **Narrative connection:** Two NPCs in the same scene who have a hidden relationship — siblings, old enemies, handler and asset — have a connection that a character with high Insight can perceive as something physical. Not an explanation, just a visible link that prompts investigation.
- **Poison or disease progression:** A healer who succeeds on a Medicine check can see the pathway of a toxin or infection through a patient's body, indicated by trace lines moving from the entry point toward vital systems. A dramatic visual for the diagnosis moment.

---

### Ritual Circle / Area Markers
*Concentric circle shapes indicating magical zones*

**In Stiltsmere:** Cult ritual circles visible at Layer 2, indicating active magical workings.

**In Any Campaign:**
- **Active spell areas:** Any persistent area-of-effect spell (*Consecrate*, *Hallow*, *Forbiddance*, *Antimagic Field*) has a visible boundary for characters with Detect Magic active. Place the circle. Other characters must be told by the detecting character where the boundary is.
- **Ritual in progress:** An ongoing ritual has a physical circle that all characters can see, but the *nature* of the ritual — what it is doing and how dangerous it is — is visible only to characters who succeed on an Arcana check. Place the circle on the base layer; add a sigil in the center for the characters who understand what they're looking at.
- **Sacred ground:** The area around a shrine, altar, or holy site has a subtle boundary perceptible to characters with the relevant religion or divine connection. Others feel vaguely uncomfortable; the cleric sees the edge of the sacred space clearly.
- **Trap area:** A magical trap covers a specific area. The rogue who spots it sees the boundary. Other characters need to be warned — and the tension of watching a character approach the edge of a circle that their companion knows about but they don't is exactly the kind of dramatic moment the system creates naturally.

---

## Building New Elements for Other Campaigns

The Stiltsmere library covers cosmic horror, fog, spectral entities, cult activity, and ancient ruins. Your other campaigns will have different needs. Here is the general principle for building new elements:

**Every element communicates one specific thing that one or more characters can perceive and others cannot.**

Good layer elements are:
- Specific enough to communicate a single clear piece of information
- Generic enough to be reusable across multiple scenes
- Small enough to fit on a standard map without dominating it
- Visually distinct from base map content at a glance

When you encounter a situation in any campaign where you think "I wish I could show the player what their character sees without just telling them," that is the signal to build a new element. The element you build for that situation will likely be useful again in the same campaign and in future ones.

Build for the situation. Keep the element. Use it forever.

---

## The Universal Starter Kit

If you want a small set of elements that will serve you across almost any campaign in almost any genre, start here. These eight elements cover the vast majority of "one character perceives something others don't" situations you will encounter at a table.

**Build once, use everywhere:**

1. **Ghost figure ×4** — any spectral presence, echo of the past, or vision
2. **Footprint trail ×3** — any tracking, invisible movement, or past passage
3. **Hidden door ×2** — any secret passage, magical concealment, or dimensional threshold
4. **Sigil ×4** — any magical marking, trap indicator, or supernatural territory marker
5. **Spectral creature token** — any invisible, illusory, or ethereal creature (blank, customisable per campaign)
6. **Thread/connection line ×4** — any magical connection, binding, or network
7. **Area marker ×2** (small and large) — any spell area, sacred ground, or ritual zone
8. **Shadow anomaly ×2** — any ambient wrongness, possession indicator, or horror element

Total build time: approximately forty-five minutes.
Total cost: one pack of transparency film and markers you already own.

This kit will be useful in your next campaign regardless of its genre, setting, or system. It travels with you. It grows with you. Every element you add to it for a specific game is an element you have permanently.

---

## A Note on Any System

This document has used D&D 5e language because Stiltsmere is a 5e campaign, but the Layer System has no system dependency whatsoever. A ghost figure placed on a map communicates the same information whether your game uses D&D, Pathfinder, Call of Cthulhu, Blades in the Dark, Mothership, or any other system. The physical element is system-agnostic. It communicates through space and transparency rather than through rules text.

If you run multiple systems across different groups, one layer library serves all of them. The investment is made once. The return compounds across every game you ever run.

---

## Summary

The Layer System is a universal DM toolkit that happens to come with a cosmic horror campaign included.

The core tools — transparent cutouts representing things only some characters can perceive, placed and removed in seconds during play — are applicable anywhere the visible world and the full world differ. Which is everywhere.

Build a library. Start small if you prefer. The minimum viable starter kit is eight element types, forty-five minutes of work, and materials you likely already own.

Use it in Stiltsmere. Use it in your next campaign. Use it in the one after that.

The layers are not a feature of one campaign. They are a way of thinking about how maps work — as base reality plus accumulated perception, showing every player what their character knows rather than telling them. Once you run one session with physical layer elements on your maps, you will not want to run without them.

---

*"Universal Layer Applications" is a companion document to "About Layers," "The DM's Layer Library," and "The Economics of Layered DMing." It is freely usable in any tabletop campaign regardless of system or setting.*

---

# The DM's Layer Library
### A Complete Reference for Every Reusable Layer Element in "The Souring of Stiltsmere"

---

## How to Use This Document

This is a build-once reference guide. Before your first session, use it to construct your complete physical layer library — every transparent cutout element the campaign will ever ask you to place. Once built, you will not need to create significant new elements until the Whispering Caves (Act II) and The City in Mercury (Act III), which have their own dedicated sections below.

Each entry includes:
- **What it looks like** — a description precise enough to draw or print from
- **Approximate size** — relative to a standard 1-inch grid map square
- **How many to make** — accounting for scenes where multiple copies appear simultaneously
- **Where it appears** — every campaign location that calls for this element
- **Tier** — which Understanding layer makes it visible
- **Notes** — any special placement or interaction guidance

Elements are organised by tier, then by type. Universal elements (used across multiple locations) are listed first within each tier. Location-specific elements follow.

---

## Colour & Opacity Reference

Before building anything, establish your colour palette. Consistency across the entire library is more important than any individual choice.

| Tier | Colour | Opacity | Effect |
|---|---|---|---|
| Layer 1 — The Crack | Silver or pale grey | 25–35% | Almost a trick of the light. Players should briefly doubt what they saw. |
| Layer 2 — The Glimpse | Cool blue-white | 45–60% | Clearly present. Clearly wrong. No room for doubt, plenty of room for dread. |
| Layer 3 — The Knowing | Deep blue-green or teal | 70–85% | Overwhelming and undeniable. The world is showing everything. |

For physical acetate cutouts, achieve opacity by layering: one sheet of marked acetate over an unmarked sheet produces approximately 40% opacity. A single sheet with heavy marker is approximately 60%. Unmarked acetate with only an outline is approximately 20%.

For digital VTT implementations, use the platform's native opacity slider set to the percentages above.

---

## Layer 1 Universal Elements
*Silver/pale grey. These appear in multiple locations throughout the campaign. Build all of these before Session 1.*

---

### L1-U1: Standing Ghost Figure
**Quantity to build:** 6
**Size:** 1 inch tall (one map square), roughly humanoid silhouette
**Appearance:** A featureless humanoid outline — no facial features, no clothing detail. Slightly blurred at the edges, as if seen through frosted glass. The figure stands with arms loosely at its sides.
**Appears in:**
- Salty Squid Inn (patrons who occasionally flicker — place 2–3 at occupied table positions)
- Gaping Maw Cannery (workers visible at Layer 1 near the crime scene)
- Whispering Caves Congregation Chamber (Area 3, seated at benches — use alongside seated variant)
- Blackwood Estate (ground floor, near covered mirrors)
- Any location where a person died or spent significant time

**Notes:** This is your most-used element. Six is the minimum — consider making eight. They are interchangeable across locations and eras of the campaign.

---

### L1-U2: Seated Ghost Figure
**Quantity to build:** 4
**Size:** 0.75 inches tall (slightly shorter than standing, implying seated posture)
**Appearance:** Same featureless silhouette as L1-U1 but with the lower body suggesting a seated position — legs at right angles, posture slightly forward as if at a table or bench.
**Appears in:**
- Salty Squid Inn (patrons at tables — Layer 1 reveals them as still present long after closing)
- Whispering Caves Congregation Chamber (benches hold spectral worshippers at Layer 1)
- Blackwood Estate study (Alistair's chair, facing the window)

**Notes:** Combine with standing figures at the same location for visual variety. A table with two seated ghosts and one standing is more unsettling than three identical seated figures.

---

### L1-U3: Pointing Ghost Figure
**Quantity to build:** 2
**Size:** 1 inch tall, arm extended at approximately 45 degrees
**Appearance:** Same featureless silhouette as L1-U1 but with one arm raised and extended, pointing. The direction of the point should be variable — make one pointing left and one pointing right, so you can orient them as needed by flipping the cutout.
**Appears in:**
- Salty Squid Inn (the spectral fisherman who points at the painting above the hearth)
- Whispering Caves (Layer 1 figures occasionally point toward hidden passages)
- Any location where a Layer 1 figure needs to direct player attention

**Notes:** This is one of the campaign's most effective single elements. A pointing ghost that indicates the hidden Fresco entrance, or a passage the party has not yet found, communicates without words and rewards the player who notices it. Place it so the point leads toward something real.

---

### L1-U4: Wet Footprint Trail
**Quantity to build:** 4 strips
**Size:** Each strip approximately 4–5 inches long, containing 4–5 individual footprints in a walking pattern
**Appearance:** Paired footprints (left and right) in a walking stride, as if someone walked through shallow water and left wet prints on the floor. The prints should be slightly smeared — not perfectly clean — and fade slightly toward one end to imply direction of travel.
**Appears in:**
- Salty Squid Inn (trail from the door to the corner where the Fresco entrance is)
- Gaping Maw Cannery (leading from Finnian's body toward the rendering vats)
- Whispering Caves Dripping Gallery (Area 2, leading toward the hidden crack in the eastern wall)
- Blackwood Estate (leading up the stairs toward Alistair's room)
- Any scene where something was present but has now left

**Notes:** Make the strips slightly curved as well as straight — a trail that turns a corner is more interesting than one that goes in a single direction. You can connect two strips at an angle to create a turning trail. Always place them so the fading end is the origin and the fresh end is the destination — players should read the direction of travel instinctively.

---

### L1-U5: Glowing Spiral Sigil
**Quantity to build:** 8
**Size:** 0.5 inches across (half a map square) for wall sigils; 1 inch across for floor sigils
**Appearance:** The cult's spiral symbol — a continuous inward spiral, hand-drawn in quality, with small branching marks at the outer edge that suggest it was made quickly but with intention. At Layer 1 it emits a faint, cold glow.
**Appears in:**
- Salty Squid Inn (above the entrance arch, visible to Tier 1 characters arriving)
- Gaping Maw Cannery (on the floor near the crime scene, the Layer 1 version glowing faintly)
- Malakai's seaside cave (multiple on the walls)
- Whispering Caves (Entrance Maw Area 1, Congregation Chamber Area 3, multiple tunnel walls)
- Cult supply routes and hidden infrastructure throughout the campaign

**Notes:** Make four at small scale (wall placement) and four at large scale (floor placement). The same physical piece can represent both by using the size that fits your map's scale at any given location. This is the cult's tag — every location they have used will have at least one.

---

### L1-U6: Faint Bloodstain
**Quantity to build:** 3
**Size:** Irregular, approximately 1–2 inches across
**Appearance:** An organic, irregular shape suggesting pooled or splattered blood that has largely dried and faded — visible at Layer 1 as a darker patch on the floor or wall, with a slightly organic edge rather than a clean geometric shape. Not vivid red — closer to a dark brown-grey at this tier.
**Appears in:**
- Gaping Maw Cannery (the spiral pattern on the floor near Finnian's body is partly constructed from his blood — Layer 1 makes the full pattern visible)
- Malakai's seaside cave (previous ritual attempts left marks that fade at Layer 0)
- Whispering Caves (various locations where rituals were performed)

**Notes:** These should feel like evidence of something that happened rather than something currently happening. Layer 1 bloodstains are archaeological. Layer 2 bloodstains (where applicable) feel recent.

---

### L1-U7: Shadow Anomaly
**Quantity to build:** 3
**Size:** Variable — shadow shapes, roughly 1–3 inches, designed to look like a shadow cast by something that is not physically present
**Appearance:** A flat shadow shape on the floor or wall, pointing in a direction inconsistent with the light source in the room. No object casting it. The shadow is vaguely humanoid but not precisely — it could be a person, or something with a person's proportions but not a person's shape.
**Appears in:**
- Salty Squid Inn (Layer 1 anomaly visible in dimly lit corners)
- Harbormaster Greaves's office (a shadow falls from his direction during conversations, slightly larger than it should be)
- Any location where the DM wants to imply a presence without showing it directly

**Notes:** Shadow anomalies are the subtlest Layer 1 element and the one most likely to generate the desired "did I just see that?" response. Place them perpendicular to actual light sources so they are visually wrong without being immediately identifiable as wrong.

---

### L1-U8: Whisper Lines
**Quantity to build:** 4
**Size:** 2–3 inches, curved lines emanating from a central point
**Appearance:** A cluster of fine curved lines radiating outward from a small central circle, like a simplified sound wave diagram or the lines around a word in a comic strip indicating speech. At Layer 1 opacity they look like faint scratches in the air.
**Appears in:**
- Whispering Caves (Entrance Maw — the acoustic effect has a Layer 1 visual component)
- Any location where voices or whispers are a Layer 1 phenomenon
- Near any Soursight anchor or cult artifact that emits psychic sound

**Notes:** These are the most abstract element in the library. They work best when placed near an object or location that the party already knows is acoustically strange — they confirm rather than introduce the phenomenon.

---

## Layer 1 Location-Specific Elements
*Build these as you approach the relevant sessions.*

---

### L1-S1: Moving Painting (Salty Squid Inn)
**Quantity to build:** 1
**Size:** Match the painting frame on your Salty Squid map exactly
**Appearance:** The same stormy sea painting that appears on the base map, but with the waves in slightly different positions — the ship tilted further, the wave crests curled in new directions. The difference should be subtle enough that a player might not immediately notice the painting has changed, but obvious once they look directly at it.
**Appears in:** Salty Squid Inn only
**Notes:** This is a static cutout placed over the painting on the base map when Layer 1 is active. The effect of a painting that has slightly moved when no one was watching is more unsettling than a painting that is visibly animated. If using VTT, consider a very slow animated GIF rather than a static image.

---

### L1-S2: Eye Shape
**Quantity to build:** 3
**Size:** 0.5 inches — small, meant to be noticed rather than dominant
**Appearance:** A simple open eye, rendered in the cult's aesthetic — an elongated oval with a circular iris and no pupil. It should look like it was drawn rather than carved. At Layer 1 opacity it appears on walls, floors, and surfaces near Watcher positions.
**Appears in:**
- Whispering Caves (near each Watcher at the Threshold position)
- Blackwood Estate (on the inside of covered mirrors, visible only if the cloth is lifted)
- Cult-adjacent locations throughout Act II

**Notes:** The eye shape is the Watcher at the Threshold's visual precursor — it marks locations where a Watcher is stationed before the Watcher itself becomes visible. A character at Tier 1 who sees the eye should feel watched. A character at Tier 1 who then sees the full Watcher understands that the eye was a warning.

---

## Layer 2 Universal Elements
*Cool blue-white. These appear in multiple locations throughout the campaign. Build all of these before Session 4 (when Act II begins in earnest).*

---

### L2-U1: Solid Ghost Figure
**Quantity to build:** 5
**Size:** 1 inch tall, same silhouette as L1-U1
**Appearance:** Same humanoid outline as the Layer 1 ghost, but significantly more opaque — this figure has weight, presence. The edges are sharper. It does not blur. It is simply there, in the room, real in the way that things at Layer 2 are real.
**Appears in:**
- Salty Squid Inn (the spectral fisherman who gestures at the painting becomes solid at Layer 2)
- Whispering Caves Congregation Chamber (the bench-ghosts solidify, become interactive)
- Any location where Layer 1 ghost figures graduate to Layer 2 presence

**Notes:** Do not replace Layer 1 ghost figures with Layer 2 ones — stack them. A location where a ghost was faint at Layer 1 and solid at Layer 2 tells a story of something becoming more real as the character's perception deepens.

---

### L2-U2: Hidden Door / Archway
**Quantity to build:** 4
**Size:** Match to your map scale — typically 1 inch wide by 2 inches tall for a standard door, larger for archways
**Appearance:** A door or archway outline, rendered in cool blue-white at Layer 2 opacity. It should look like light is coming through it — not bright light, but a different quality of light than the surrounding area. The frame of the door is visible; the space beyond it is a slightly lighter opacity suggesting depth.
**Appears in:**
- Salty Squid Inn (the Weeping Fresco entrance — a doorway in what appeared to be a solid wall)
- Whispering Caves (multiple hidden passages throughout Areas 2–9)
- Blackwood Estate (passage behind the study bookshelf)
- The City in Mercury (the Tesseract Labyrinth exit archway, repositioned as symbols are pressed)

**Notes:** This is structurally the most important element in the entire library. A hidden door cutout placed against a wall section changes the physical geography of the encounter. It should be placed with deliberate care — the position of the archway relative to the map tells the party where to go. Make one that is clearly a doorway shape and one that is more of a ragged opening for different aesthetic contexts.

---

### L2-U3: Hidden Staircase
**Quantity to build:** 2
**Size:** 2–3 inches long, staircase shape (stepped profile)
**Appearance:** A staircase rendered in blue-white, descending or ascending from a point that at Layer 0 appeared to be a floor or wall. The steps should be clear and countable — five to seven steps visible before the staircase disappears into a darkness that implies continuation.
**Appears in:**
- Whispering Caves (descent passages between areas that appear blocked at Layer 0)
- The City in Mercury (passages in the Resonator Chamber that appear as bare walls at lower tiers)

**Notes:** Make one staircase descending (steps going away from the viewer, smaller as they recede) and one ascending (steps coming toward the viewer, larger as they approach). The descending version is used more frequently — the campaign's significant discoveries are mostly below ground level.

---

### L2-U4: Organic Conduit Trace
**Quantity to build:** 6 segments
**Size:** Each segment 3–4 inches long, slightly curved, approximately 0.25 inches wide
**Appearance:** A vein-like channel, slightly irregular in width, with a faint bioluminescent glow along its length. It should look organic rather than mechanical — more like a blood vessel than a pipe. The glow pulses slightly (at Layer 2 opacity this can be suggested by making the centre of the conduit slightly brighter than the edges).
**Appears in:**
- Whispering Caves (the bioluminescent network visible throughout the entire cave system at Layer 2, running along walls and ceilings, connecting every chamber)

**Notes:** These segments should be connectable end-to-end to create longer runs across the map. Make them curved so they can be arranged to flow around the cave's natural architecture. The network they create should look like it has a destination — all conduits should point generally toward the deeper sections of the cave, eventually converging on the Petrified Heart chamber.

---

### L2-U5: Ritual Circle
**Quantity to build:** 3 (at different scales)
**Size:** Small (1 inch diameter), medium (2 inch diameter), large (3 inch diameter)
**Appearance:** Concentric circles with geometric markings between the rings — the cult's script or The City in Mercury symbols rendered as angular, precise geometric marks. At Layer 2 the circle glows faintly and the space at its centre appears slightly darker than it should.
**Appears in:**
- Whispering Caves (the void-circle in the Deep Ritual Hall, Area 9 — use the large version)
- Gaping Maw Cannery (the Cannery Horror's origin ritual, still faintly visible at Layer 2 after the creature's creation)
- Malakai's seaside cave (the incomplete ritual circle)
- The City in Mercury (various ritual spaces in the Resonator Chamber)

**Notes:** The large ritual circle cutout for Area 9 of the Whispering Caves is one of the campaign's most dramatic placements. Set it in the centre of the map for the Deep Ritual Hall encounter and every player at the table — regardless of their character's tier — should feel its presence.

---

### L2-U6: Soursight Face
**Quantity to build:** 5
**Size:** 0.75 inches — slightly smaller than a map square
**Appearance:** A distorted face shape with the mouth open in a wide, silent expression. The features are recognisable as a face but slightly wrong — eyes too wide, jaw slightly too long, the expression somewhere between surprise and hunger. At Layer 2 opacity these appear in reflective surfaces, in shadows, in the water of the Glimmer Fields.
**Appears in:**
- Salty Squid Inn (faces visible in the pools in the Dripping Gallery at Layer 2)
- Isle of Reeds (faces in the water of the flooded fields during night scenes)
- Any scene where ambient Soursight pressure should be felt as a Layer 2 visual element

**Notes:** These faces should never be placed looking directly at the viewer — always slightly off-angle, as if the face is aware of being seen and has just begun to turn. The almost-looking-at-you quality is significantly more unsettling than direct eye contact.

---

## Layer 2 Location-Specific Elements
*Build these as you approach the relevant sessions.*

---

### L2-S1: Grotto Entrance (Salty Squid Inn)
**Quantity to build:** 1
**Size:** Approximately 2 inches wide by 3 inches tall — large enough to be immediately visible on the map
**Appearance:** A ragged opening in what was a solid wall, with rough stone edges and a tunnel visible beyond — a swirling, almost liquid quality to the light within, as if the passage itself is uncertain about its own geometry. This is the entrance to the Weeping Fresco.
**Appears in:** Salty Squid Inn corner wall only
**Notes:** This is the Shattering Event's visual payoff. Place it immediately when the Shattering Event occurs. The entrance should feel like the wall has opened a mouth — appropriately thematic. It disappears when Sanity is restored to 50 or higher.

---

### L2-S2: Reflected Stalker Token
**Quantity to build:** 1 per PC (print at 40–50% opacity)
**Size:** Standard creature token size for your map scale
**Appearance:** A standard creature token for each player character — ideally matching their actual tokens — printed or rendered at reduced opacity and with a slight silver tint. The reflection looks like the character but slightly off: posture subtly wrong, expression slightly blank.
**Appears in:** Any encounter where a Reflected Stalker appears
**Notes:** These are the campaign's most personalised elements. If possible, base them on the actual tokens your players use for their characters. The moment a player sees their own character's token placed on the map at reduced opacity is the moment the Reflected Stalker mechanic becomes viscerally real rather than abstractly threatening.

---

### L2-S3: Fossilised Rib Curve (Whispering Caves Chasm, Area 6)
**Quantity to build:** 4
**Size:** 3–4 inches long, curved, suggesting the arc of a very large rib
**Appearance:** A massive curved bone shape visible in the walls of the chasm — the scale should feel wrong, too large to be any creature the players know. At Layer 2 the bone is clearly visible as bone rather than stone, with faint striations along its length.
**Appears in:** Whispering Caves chasm area (Area 6) and surrounding tunnel walls
**Notes:** Place these on the sides of the chasm map when Layer 2 is active, curving away into the darkness. This is the first visible hint of the Layer 3 revelation — the party sees bone in the rock, recognises it as bone, and has no framework yet to understand its scale. Do not explain it. Let it sit.

---

## Layer 3 Universal Elements
*Deep blue-green/teal. These appear primarily in the Whispering Caves and The City in Mercury. Build them before Session 7.*

---

### L3-U1: Petrified Anatomy Indicators
**Quantity to build:** 6 assorted
**Size:** Varies — some 2 inches (smaller anatomical features), some 4–5 inches (major structures)
**Appearance:** Large-scale organic forms that are recognisable as anatomy — curved rib sections, cylindrical structures suggesting a trachea or major vessel, flat sheet-like structures suggesting fascia or membrane — all rendered in deep teal at high opacity, clearly present rather than suggested.
**Appears in:**
- Whispering Caves throughout (every area reveals a different anatomical feature at Layer 3 — the corridors are intestinal passages, the chambers are organ cavities, the crystals are calcified cilia)
- The chasm (full ribcage visible at Layer 3, the rib curves from L2-S3 now joined into a complete structure)

**Notes:** These are your most significant worldbuilding elements. Placed collectively across the Whispering Caves map, they should make the cave system's true nature unmistakable to a Tier 3 character. The effect should be that of suddenly seeing the image in a Magic Eye poster — once the anatomy is visible, the cave cannot be unseen as a cave. It is a body.

---

### L3-U2: Energy Conduit Network (Full)
**Quantity to build:** 1 large composite piece
**Size:** Sized to cover the full Whispering Caves map with a network of connecting lines
**Appearance:** The complete bioluminescent conduit network — all the Layer 2 segments (L2-U4) now visible simultaneously and connected, forming a full vascular map of the cave system. At Layer 3 opacity the network glows visibly, and the direction of flow is apparent — everything moves toward the Petrified Heart chamber.
**Appears in:** Whispering Caves (the full cave map, as a single composite overlay)
**Notes:** This is the one element in the library that works better as a full sheet than as individual cutouts, because its value is in showing the whole network simultaneously. Consider making this as a single transparency sheet rather than individual segments — it is the Layer 3 moment where the cave system reveals itself as a single organism, and the visual impact requires the whole picture at once.

---

### L3-U3: Cosmic String Lines
**Quantity to build:** 6 strips
**Size:** 3–4 inches, very thin lines, slightly curved
**Appearance:** Extremely fine lines, barely visible, connecting points on the map as if showing the tension between objects — the strings of reality that a Tier 3 character can perceive. At Layer 3 opacity they should be clearly intentional rather than accidental, but not bright — more like seeing the grain of something than seeing the thing itself.
**Appears in:**
- Any Tier 3 location where the party needs to perceive the connections between objects or entities
- The City in Mercury (the energy conduits between the Resonator and the pylons)
- The Echo of the Maw final encounter (connecting the Ritual Anchors to the central manifestation)

**Notes:** Cosmic string lines are primarily a puzzle tool rather than an atmospheric one. When placed between the Ritual Anchors in the Echo of the Maw encounter, they show Tier 3 characters exactly what needs to be severed. Place them only when there is something specific they should communicate.

---

### L3-U4: Reality Fracture Lines
**Quantity to build:** 4
**Size:** 2–4 inches, jagged crack shapes
**Appearance:** Sharp, angular crack shapes that look like fractures in glass or stone but with a slight luminescence along the break line, as if light is visible through a crack in the wall of reality. At Layer 3 they are clearly not physical cracks — no rubble, no displaced material. The crack is in something invisible.
**Appears in:**
- The Petrified Heart chamber (the air around the Heart shows fracture lines at Layer 3, indicating the wound the Fracture left)
- The City in Mercury Resonator Chamber (fractures visible in the air around the damaged crystal)
- The final confrontation (the Echo of the Maw's presence creates visible fracture lines in the air around it)

**Notes:** Reality fracture lines communicate that something fundamental is broken in this location. Use them sparingly — three or four fracture lines in a scene are powerful; a dozen become wallpaper.

---

### L3-U5: Deep Shaft Indicator
**Quantity to build:** 1
**Size:** 2 inches diameter
**Appearance:** A dark circle with implied depth — concentric rings getting darker toward the centre, as if looking down into something very deep. The darkness at the centre should be absolute.
**Appears in:** Whispering Caves Deep Ritual Hall (Area 9) — the void-circle at Layer 3 reveals itself as a shaft descending hundreds of feet rather than a floor-level object
**Notes:** Place this over or alongside the Layer 2 ritual circle (L2-U5) when Layer 3 is active. The ritual circle is the cult's construction; the shaft beneath it is what the ritual circle was built to access. A character at Tier 3 looking at the overlaid elements sees both simultaneously — the ritual above, the depth below.

---

## The City in Mercury Specific Elements
*Build these before Session 10 (if the party pursues the prestige dungeon).*

---

### CiM-1: Tesseract Labyrinth Exit Archway
**Quantity to build:** 1 (repositionable)
**Size:** Match to your Tesseract Labyrinth map scale — a standard doorway
**Appearance:** A blue-white archway that looks like the others in the labyrinth but glows slightly more intensely — it is the aligned exit, and its alignment makes it visually distinct.
**Placement notes:** This is the puzzle's physical component. As the party presses different symbols on the dodecahedron crystal, move this cutout to different wall positions on the map. The correct three-symbol sequence positions it at the map's designated exit wall. Track which wall positions correspond to which symbol combinations before the session.

---

### CiM-2: Obsidian Sentinel "Dormant" Indicator
**Quantity to build:** 2
**Size:** Match to your Obsidian Sentinel token
**Appearance:** The Obsidian Sentinel token at very low opacity (15–20%) — barely visible, indistinguishable from the plaza statues at Layer 0. This indicates which statues are actually Sentinels before they activate.
