**Placement notes:** Place these on the two statue positions that will become active Sentinels when the party crosses the plaza's midpoint. At Layer 0 they are imperceptible. A character at Layer 2 or higher may notice that two statues have a slightly different quality to them — slightly more present than the others. A DC 17 Perception check at Layer 2 allows a player to identify the dormant Sentinels before they activate, giving the party the option to approach differently.

---

### CiM-3: Resonator Energy Conduits
**Quantity to build:** 3 (one per pylon)
**Size:** 4–5 inches, arcing from the pylon position to the central Resonator
**Appearance:** Bright arcs of blue-white energy, significantly more opaque than the Whispering Caves conduit network — these are active power conduits, not biological vessels. They pulse visibly and crackle at their edges.
**Placement notes:** Place these when the Resonator Chamber encounter begins. Each conduit connects one of the three pylons to the central crystal. When a pylon is destroyed, remove the corresponding conduit. The visual simplification of the Resonator's power should be apparent as pylons fall.

---

## Quick Reference: Complete Library Checklist

### Before Session 1
- [ ] L1-U1: Standing Ghost Figure ×6
- [ ] L1-U2: Seated Ghost Figure ×4
- [ ] L1-U3: Pointing Ghost Figure ×2
- [ ] L1-U4: Wet Footprint Trail ×4 strips
- [ ] L1-U5: Glowing Spiral Sigil ×8 (4 small, 4 large)
- [ ] L1-U6: Faint Bloodstain ×3
- [ ] L1-U7: Shadow Anomaly ×3
- [ ] L1-U8: Whisper Lines ×4
- [ ] L1-S1: Moving Painting (Salty Squid Inn) ×1
- [ ] L1-S2: Eye Shape ×3

### Before Session 4
- [ ] L2-U1: Solid Ghost Figure ×5
- [ ] L2-U2: Hidden Door / Archway ×4
- [ ] L2-U3: Hidden Staircase ×2
- [ ] L2-U4: Organic Conduit Trace ×6 segments
- [ ] L2-U5: Ritual Circle ×3 (small, medium, large)
- [ ] L2-U6: Soursight Face ×5
- [ ] L2-S1: Grotto Entrance (Salty Squid Inn) ×1
- [ ] L2-S2: Reflected Stalker Token ×1 per PC
- [ ] L2-S3: Fossilised Rib Curve ×4

### Before Session 7
- [ ] L3-U1: Petrified Anatomy Indicators ×6 assorted
- [ ] L3-U2: Energy Conduit Network (Full) ×1 composite sheet
- [ ] L3-U3: Cosmic String Lines ×6 strips
- [ ] L3-U4: Reality Fracture Lines ×4
- [ ] L3-U5: Deep Shaft Indicator ×1

### Before Session 10 (The City in Mercury only)
- [ ] CiM-1: Tesseract Labyrinth Exit Archway ×1
- [ ] CiM-2: Obsidian Sentinel Dormant Indicator ×2
- [ ] CiM-3: Resonator Energy Conduits ×3

---

## Element Storage and Organisation

A small accordion folder with five sections works well for physical libraries:

- **Section 1:** Layer 1 Universal (L1-U elements)
- **Section 2:** Layer 1 Location-Specific (L1-S elements)
- **Section 3:** Layer 2 (all L2 elements)
- **Section 4:** Layer 3 (all L3 elements)
- **Section 5:** The City in Mercury (all CiM elements)

Store the full library in your DM bag alongside the main campaign guide. At the table, pull the relevant section when a threshold is crossed rather than sorting through the whole library mid-session. Once you have run the system for two or three sessions it becomes entirely automatic.

---

*"The DM's Layer Library" is a companion document to "The Souring of Stiltsmere: A Campaign Guide" and "About Layers." It should be used alongside the About Layers document, which explains the physical implementation methods for each element type.*

---

# A DM's Primer to Stiltsmere
### How to Run "The Souring of Stiltsmere"

---

## Who This Document Is For

This primer is written for a Dungeon Master who is encountering "The Souring of Stiltsmere" for the first time and wants to understand the campaign's design philosophy before reading the full guide. It is also useful for a DM who has read the full guide but wants a concise reference for what makes this campaign structurally different from standard 5e adventures and how to approach running it well.

The campaign guide is comprehensive. This document is a lens for reading it.

---

## The Central Design Principle: Show, Don't Tell

Every major mechanic in this campaign is introduced through experience rather than explanation. Players are never given a rulebook speech about how Sanity works, what the Understanding Tiers mean, or what the layers of the world contain. They discover these things by living through their consequences.

This is intentional and it should guide how you present information at the table. When a player's Sanity drops and they cross into a new Understanding Tier, you do not announce "you have now reached Tier 1." You place the first transparent overlay on the map and let them see that there are things in the room that weren't there before. The mechanic explains itself.

The campaign's opening is a song, not a setting description. The Weeping Fresco tutorial dungeon teaches the Mender inversion not through rules text but by healing a player out of the layer where they can perceive the danger. The Shattering Event that unlocks the Fresco happens to the party rather than being announced in advance. Every system in this campaign is built on this principle: **the world demonstrates its own rules.**

Your job as DM is to trust this approach and resist the impulse to explain. Let the mechanics land. Let the confusion resolve into understanding organically. It will.

---

## The Two Systems You Need to Internalise Before Session One

### 1. The Sanity/Understanding System

Every player character begins with **100 Sanity** and **Understanding Tier 0**. As Sanity decreases, Understanding increases through five tiers. This is not a punishment mechanic. It is a perception mechanic.

The crucial thing to hold onto: **higher Understanding is not worse, it is different.** A character at Tier 3 with 15 Sanity remaining is not a character who is losing. They are a character who sees more of the world than anyone else at the table, at the cost of finding it increasingly difficult to communicate that vision to people who don't share it. This tension — between the value of what is perceived and the cost of perceiving it — is what the campaign is about.

The five tiers in brief:

| Tier | Sanity Range | What Changes |
|---|---|---|
| 0 — The Veil | 100–75 | Nothing. The world is as it seems. |
| 1 — The Crack | 74–50 | First layer visible. Supernatural ambushes sensed. Minor apparitions. |
| 2 — The Glimpse | 49–25 | Second layer visible. Hidden spaces accessible. DM may present false information. |
| 3 — The Knowing | 24–1 | Third layer visible. Cosmic machinery visible. Quest for The City in Mercury unlocks. Social penalties with Tier 0 NPCs. |
| 4 — The Unraveling | 0 | Character becomes an NPC. Not hostile necessarily, but no longer playable. |

The most important enemy in the campaign is the **Whispering Mender** — a creature whose primary action *heals* Sanity. When Sanity is healed past a tier threshold, a character is ejected from the layer where they can perceive the creatures and content of that tier. This means healing is sometimes the threat. This will feel strange to players the first time it happens. It will feel like genius the second time.

### 2. The Layered Map System

Prepare your key location maps as a base layer plus up to three transparent overlays, one per Understanding Tier. Each overlay adds content visible only to characters at that tier or higher: new enemies, new clues, new paths, new entire rooms.

The critical point that is easy to miss: **layers don't just add enemies and clues. They add geography.** A wall at Layer 0 can be a doorway at Layer 2. A collapsed building at Tier 1 can have a spectral staircase at Tier 2. Players at higher Understanding Tiers can access physical locations that are simply not there for lower-tier characters. This is the mechanic's most powerful application and the one that requires the most preparation investment — but it is also what makes the system feel like genuine revelation rather than a vision effect.

At the table, place the base map. When a character crosses a tier threshold, place the corresponding overlay. All players can see it out-of-character — this is intentional. The dramatic irony of a Tier 0 character watching their Tier 2 companion fight something invisible is a feature, not a problem. It creates roleplay pressure and raises the emotional stakes of Sanity loss for the whole table, not just the affected player.

---

## The Shattering Event: The Campaign's Most Important Single Moment

The Weeping Fresco is the campaign's tutorial dungeon, teaching the Mender inversion and the layer ejection mechanic in a controlled environment. But the Fresco requires Understanding Tier 2 to enter — which creates a problem, because players need to reach Tier 2 before they have any experience of what Tier 2 means.

The solution is the **Shattering Event**: a scripted moment of collective horror that drops the entire party to exactly 49 Sanity simultaneously, crossing the Tier 1/Tier 2 threshold in a single shared experience.

**When it happens:** After the party's first major confrontation with cult evidence — the cannery crime scene is ideal, or after defeating Malakai and witnessing the attempted sacrifice. The party returns to the inn that night.

**What happens:** Something occurs in the inn — a Soursight through the window, a sound from behind the painting above the hearth, a moment where every reflection in the room turns to face them simultaneously — that forces a Sanity saving throw the party is designed to fail. All party members drop to exactly 49 Sanity. The second-layer overlay goes on the map. The crawlspace in the corner wall becomes visible.

**Why it matters:** This moment does four things simultaneously. It synchronises the party's Understanding Tiers so everyone crosses the threshold together. It makes the inn — the designated safe space — feel compromised for the first time. It unlocks the Fresco at the dramatically correct moment. And it teaches players that the campaign's horror is not confined to dungeons and encounters. It happens in the room where they sleep.

Do not telegraph this moment. Do not explain it after it happens. Place the overlay and describe what they see. Let the mechanic teach itself.

---

## The Campaign's Three-Act Structure

**Act I (Sessions 1–3): The Murder at the Gaping Maw**
The party arrives as investigators. The goal is legible: find who killed Finnian. This act exists to establish the setting, the NPCs, and a sense of normalcy before it begins to erode. The Shattering Event ends this act. Do not rush it — the more comfortable the party becomes in Stiltsmere before the Shattering Event, the harder that moment lands.

Key embedded hook: **The cannery office ledger connecting the Blackwood family to the cult.** This is structural to Elara Vane's full character arc and should be findable during the normal murder investigation. Do not hide it behind an unreasonably high check.

**Act II (Sessions 4–8): The Whispering Cult**
The investigation expands across all three islands. The party discovers the cult's infrastructure, its leadership, and its plan. This act contains the campaign's most varied content — the Isle of Reeds' environmental horror, the Whispering Caves dungeon, the Blackwood Estate investigation, the three side quests that reward thorough exploration with animal companions and island-specific tools.

The Whispering Caves is the act's centrepiece. It contains the cult triumvirate, the full Layer 3 revelation about the petrified creature, the resolution of Subplot C (Leo), and Ossian's potential surrender — one of the campaign's richest roleplay moments if the party has done their homework.

**Act III (Sessions 9+): The Souring**
Three ritual sites. A celestial alignment. A time limit the party can feel. This act is a pressure cooker, and it should feel like one. The City in Mercury prestige dungeon is available now for parties that have triggered it — it is a detour that makes the final confrontation easier, not a requirement for reaching it.

The Echo of the Maw is a two-phase fight that requires Understanding Tier 2 to meaningfully engage with. This is intentional. By the final act, the party's Sanity management decisions across the entire campaign determine how equipped they are for the final battle. Earlier choices have structural consequences here.

---

## Running the NPCs

**Elara Vane** is the campaign's most complex character and is easily underplayed if you treat her as a quest-dispenser. She knows more than she says from the beginning. She is managing the party, managing her family's exposure, and managing her own horror at what she has discovered about the Blackwood patriarch. Play her as someone carrying weight she hasn't decided to share yet. The moment she does share it — when confronted with the ledger — is one of the campaign's best potential scenes if the table has invested in her as a person rather than a contractor.

**Harbormaster Greaves** should be helpful to the point of mild irritation in Acts I and II. He is overworked. He wants this solved quietly. He gently steers the investigation away from anything that might implicate the cult. None of this should look like villainy — it should look like an overwhelmed official trying to manage a crisis. His reveal works in proportion to how thoroughly you have established his ordinary competence.

**Silas** is the party's emotional anchor. Use him. His grandson Leo's disappearance (Subplot C) gives him stakes beyond transportation, and his song in the opening vignette is the campaign's thesis statement in four verses. If the party rescues Leo, Silas's gratitude should feel like one of the campaign's genuine human victories.

**Ossian (Voice of the Mountain)** should not feel like a villain when the party reaches him. His private journal reveals a man who understood too late what he had done. If the party has read that journal before the confrontation, there is a conversation available that is more interesting than the fight. Reward parties who took the time to find it.

**Senne (Tender of the Bloom)** has a redemption arc built into her stat block. Use it. A party that flips her to an ally gains intelligence, an antidote to the cult's tincture distribution, and a relationship with a character who is genuinely trying to find a way out of something she walked into without full information. She is also the campaign's best argument that the cult is not simply evil — it is people who made decisions and then couldn't unmake them.

---

## The Bestiary: What Makes These Creatures Different

The creatures in this campaign are designed as a tactical ecosystem rather than a collection of individual threats. Before running any encounter, read the **Synergies** section of each creature involved. The campaign's most interesting combat moments emerge from creature combinations, not from individual stat blocks.

Three creatures deserve special attention before your first session:

**The Whispering Mender** (creature 5) — The campaign's most mechanically unusual creature. Its primary action heals Sanity, which ejects characters from higher Understanding layers. This will confuse players the first time it happens. The Weeping Fresco exists specifically to teach this lesson in a low-stakes environment before it appears in a high-stakes one. Run the Fresco before the Mender appears anywhere else.

**The Reflected Stalker** (creature 3) — Only visible to characters at Understanding Tier 1 or higher. A character at Tier 0 watching their companion fight empty air is not a bug. It is the intended experience. Let it play out. The dramatic irony of one player knowing what is happening and being unable to make their Tier 0 companion understand it is some of the best roleplay this campaign generates.

**The Echo of the Maw** (creature 30) — Read the transparency note before your final session. The Reality-Devouring Gaze can permanently erase a character from existence. This should be foreshadowed before the final confrontation, not encountered as a surprise. The session zero guide covers this but reinforce it in-world during Act III.

---

## The Three Endings and How to Run Them

The campaign has three possible endings and they are all valid. Do not steer the party toward one.

**The Golden Ending** (all rituals disrupted) is a victory that costs something. The party is changed, their Sanity depleted, their understanding of the world permanently expanded beyond what ordinary life can contain. Play the aftermath as quietly as the climax was loud. They saved Stiltsmere. They cannot go back to who they were.

**The Pyrrhic Ending** (one or two rituals disrupted) is a partial victory that becomes a new status quo. Stiltsmere survives but is transformed — Soursights are now a nightly occurrence, the fog is more aggressive, the islands are a fortified and terrified community. The party succeeded at something. It was not enough. Both things are true.

**The Failure Ending** is not a bad session. It is a different campaign outcome with its own emotional weight and its own forward momentum. The party escapes on Silas's boat with three months before the Shine spreads beyond the Lake of Tears. They have the evidence, the knowledge, and the scars. The next campaign begins here if the group wants to continue.

Whichever ending the party reaches, the campaign's final image should be chosen with care. The last thing players remember will define how they carry this experience. Give it the weight it deserves.

---

## Practical Table Management Notes

**Sound matters more than most DMs expect in this campaign.** If your table uses ambient audio, a track of lapping water, distant wind, and occasional unidentifiable low tones will do more atmospheric work than any amount of descriptive text. The absence of sound in The City in Mercury should be viscerally noticeable.

**Track Sanity visibly.** Give players a physical token, a notecard, a dial — something they can hold and see and manipulate. Sanity is only a meaningful mechanic if players feel its movement. Abstract tracking on a character sheet buries the experience.

**The Random Fog Events table is a pacing tool, not a random encounter table.** Use it when the session needs an injection of atmosphere or when travel between locations risks feeling like a loading screen. The best fog events are the ones that create a beat of dread without requiring a resolution — the Follower, the Silent Choir, the Cold Spot. They remind players that the world is active even when nothing is happening.

**Reward thoroughness without requiring it.** The campaign is fully completable without the animal companion quests, The City in Mercury, the full Subplot chain, or the Ossian negotiation. Players who explore deeply get more — better tools, richer context, more satisfying endings. Players who move with purpose get a complete and coherent experience. Design your session pacing around both possibilities simultaneously.

---

## A Final Note on Tone

This campaign is about the cost of knowing things. Every mechanic, every NPC arc, every dungeon and creature and item in it is ultimately in service of that single idea. When in doubt about any ruling, any description, any moment of ambiguity — ask yourself which choice better serves that theme.

The players came to understand something. The world is going to let them. It is going to cost them. And somewhere in the space between what they lose and what they gain, the campaign will find its meaning.

That meaning will be different for every group. That is the point.

*Good luck. Trust the design. Trust your players.*

*The boat leaves at dusk.*

---

*"A DM's Primer to Stiltsmere" is a companion document to "The Souring of Stiltsmere: A Campaign Guide." Both are free for personal and table use.*

---

# Sanity & Scaling Guide
### Session-by-Session Progression for "The Souring of Stiltsmere"

---

## What This Document Is

This guide gives Dungeon Masters a concrete framework for tracking the campaign's two primary progression gradients: **Sanity/Understanding** and **character level**. It provides session-by-session benchmarks for where the party should approximately be, tools for reading whether they are ahead of or behind the intended gradient, and specific techniques for nudging the pacing in either direction without disrupting the story.

This is a guide, not a script. The benchmarks are targets, not requirements. Every table moves differently and the campaign is designed to accommodate that — but having a sense of the intended gradient allows you to make informed decisions about when to apply pressure and when to ease it.

---

## Understanding the Two Gradients

### Gradient 1: Sanity/Understanding

The campaign's emotional and perceptual arc runs from 100 Sanity (full consensus reality, no layer access) to somewhere in the range of 20–50 Sanity by the final act (partial or deep layer access, significant Understanding). Very few characters should reach 0 Sanity before the final confrontation — Tier 4 conversion is an ending, not a mid-campaign event.

The intended gradient is **gradual in Act I, accelerating in Act II, and steep in Act III**. Players should feel relatively grounded in their first few sessions, begin to notice the cracks in Act II, and experience genuine psychological pressure in Act III.

The Shattering Event is the campaign's single deliberate disruption of this gradient — a controlled spike that moves the entire party to exactly 49 Sanity at a dramatically chosen moment, synchronising their tiers for the Weeping Fresco tutorial. After the Shattering Event, Sanity restoration opportunities should be available to bring players back up to the 55–70 range before Act II begins in earnest, ensuring they have room to descend again meaningfully.

### Gradient 2: Character Level

The campaign is designed for characters beginning at **Level 3** and reaching approximately **Level 9–10** by the final confrontation. This maps well onto the D&D 5e tier structure: the early acts use Tier 1 challenges (Levels 1–4), Act II uses Tier 2 (Levels 5–10), and the prestige dungeon and final confrontation are designed for the upper end of Tier 2.

The two gradients are not independent. Understanding Tier progression and character level progression should feel loosely synchronised — a character who has the mechanical power of Level 7 should also have enough Understanding to perceive the true nature of what they are fighting. A party that levels too quickly relative to their Sanity loss will feel like they are outpacing the horror. A party that loses Sanity too quickly relative to their level will struggle with the mechanical demands of high-layer encounters.

---

## Session-by-Session Benchmarks

The following table assumes approximately 3–4 hours per session and a party of 3–5 players at moderate investigation pace. Adjust for your table's speed.

| Session | Act | Expected Sanity Range | Expected Level | Key Milestone |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | I | 85–100 | 3 | Arrival, Silas's song, first impressions of Stiltsmere |
| 2 | I | 70–90 | 3 | Crime scene investigation, Malakai's trail, first Soursight encounter |
| 3 | I | **49 (Shattering Event)** | 3–4 | Malakai confrontation, Shattering Event, Weeping Fresco available |
| 4 | I–II | 50–65 | 4 | Fresco attempted (ejection expected), Elara confrontation with ledger, Act II begins |
| 5 | II | 45–60 | 4–5 | Isle of Reeds introduction, Blighted Bloom quest, submerged shrine |
| 6 | II | 40–55 | 5 | Isle of Crags introduction, Lost Herd quest, Whispering Caves entrance |
| 7 | II | 30–50 | 5–6 | Whispering Caves (Areas 1–6), first Ossian encounter possible |
| 8 | II | 25–45 | 6 | Whispering Caves (Areas 7–11), cult triumvirate confrontation, Leo rescued |
| 9 | II–III | 20–40 | 6–7 | Cult plan fully revealed, celestial alignment timeline established, Act III begins |
| 10 | III | 15–35 | 7 | First ritual site disruption, The City in Mercury quest triggered (if applicable) |
| 11 | III | 10–30 | 7–8 | The City in Mercury (if pursued) or second ritual site disruption |
| 12 | III | 5–25 | 8–9 | Second/third ritual site disruption, approach to final confrontation |
| 13+ | III | 1–20 | 9–10 | Echo of the Maw final confrontation, ending |

**Reading the table:** The left column of each Sanity range represents a party that has engaged thoroughly with horror content and taken significant Sanity damage. The right column represents a more cautious party that has avoided some encounters and used restoration methods consistently. Both are valid positions — the range accommodates different playstyles.

---

## The Shattering Event in Detail

Session 3 is the campaign's most mechanically choreographed moment. Here is how to execute it cleanly.

**The setup:** By the end of Session 2, the party has investigated the crime scene, found the cloth, traced Malakai's trail, and probably done significant work on the murder investigation. They return to the Salty Squid after the Malakai confrontation (or the crime scene visit, if you prefer that trigger).

**The execution:** The party settles in for the night. Give them a moment of relative comfort — Brenna cleaning glasses, the fire crackling, a round of drinks. Then:

Something happens. The exact nature of the Shattering Event is yours to choose based on what will hit hardest for your specific group. Options:

- Every reflection in the inn — the glasses, the windows, the puddles of spilled ale — simultaneously shows the party the same thing: themselves, standing still, mouths open, staring back.
- The painting of the stormy sea above the hearth begins to move. Then every patron in the inn slowly turns to look at it. Then turns back. Mouths open. As if nothing happened.
- The inn goes silent. Not quiet — silent. No fire crackle, no ambient sound. Every character hears, perfectly clearly, the sound of their own heartbeat. Then a second heartbeat, slightly out of sync. Then a third.

Whatever you choose, frame it as a collective experience — every party member is affected simultaneously. The saving throw is designed to fail. Every character drops to exactly **49 Sanity**.

Place the Layer 2 overlays on the Salty Squid map. The crawlspace in the corner becomes visible. Say nothing about what it is. Let them find it.

**After the event:** The party should have access to at least one Sanity restoration opportunity before the next major encounter — ideally a full night's rest in relative safety plus a Glimmer Bloom Vial or similar. This brings them back to approximately 55–65 Sanity, ensuring they begin Act II with room to descend through Tier 2 meaningfully rather than immediately pressing toward Tier 3.

---

## Reading the Gradient: Is Your Party On Track?

### Signs the party is moving too fast through Sanity loss

- Characters are reaching Tier 3 before Act II is complete
- Players are making jokes about their character's madness rather than engaging with it seriously (a sign the mechanic has lost its weight)
- The party is avoiding combat and investigation to prevent further Sanity loss, stalling the story
- One or more characters are approaching 0 Sanity before the final act

**Correction tools:**
- Increase the availability of restoration opportunities. Add a Glimmer Bloom vendor to a location the party visits. Have Elara provide her Steadying Draughts earlier.
- Reduce Sanity save DCs for ambient encounters (fog events, minor Soursights) by 2–3 while keeping combat-specific DCs stable.
- Introduce the "High Places" restoration locations (described in Part XIII of the main guide) explicitly — have an NPC mention the mountain spring or the lighthouse view as a place "where the Shine seems lighter somehow."
- Trigger the Moment of Clarity fog event and frame it generously — allow the DC 12 Wisdom check to succeed more easily for a session.

### Signs the party is moving too slowly through Sanity loss

- Characters are at Act II with Sanity above 80, still in Tier 0
- The layer system is invisible — players haven't experienced any tier-based content yet
- The campaign feels like a conventional murder mystery without horror register
- Players seem bored between combat encounters

**Correction tools:**
- Increase the frequency and intensity of Soursight encounters during travel between locations.
- Add a Whispering Mender encounter but have it target Sanity restoration on a character who is already at low Sanity, demonstrating the ejection mechanic to the whole table.
- Trigger the Shattering Event earlier if it hasn't occurred — move it to the end of Session 2 rather than Session 3.
- Introduce a Reflected Stalker encounter. Its Layer-Bound trait, combined with one character being at Tier 1 and others at Tier 0, creates immediate pressure to engage with the tier system.
- Have an NPC — Silas, Brenna, a Crag-herder guide — describe a Soursight they experienced in vivid personal terms. First-person description of the open-mouth phenomenon is more effective than most mechanical encounters at generating voluntary Sanity engagement.

### Signs the leveling pace is misaligned with Sanity progression

**Party is overleveled for their Sanity range:** They have too much mechanical power relative to their perceptual access. High-level spells are trivialising encounters that should feel dangerous. Increase encounter complexity — add layer-specific enemies that require Understanding to fight effectively, making the party's Sanity investment matter mechanically.

**Party is underleveled for their Sanity range:** They are perceiving threats they lack the resources to address. Be cautious with this scenario — it can produce genuine player frustration rather than productive tension. Reduce the aggression of layer-visible enemies temporarily, and ensure restoration opportunities are accessible. A brief "stabilisation session" focused on investigation and NPC interaction rather than combat can level the party without disrupting the Sanity gradient.

---

## Understanding Tier Milestones: What Each Threshold Should Feel Like

### Crossing into Tier 1 (Sanity 74)

This should feel like something is slightly wrong with the world rather than dramatically different. The first Layer 1 element the player sees should be subtle — a pair of footprints that weren't there before, a ghost glimpsed across a room that looks away when noticed. The player should not be certain whether they actually saw something or imagined it.

This uncertainty is the correct experience. Do not confirm what they saw. Let the ambiguity sit.

**DM action at this threshold:** Place one or two small Layer 1 cutouts in the current location. A ghost figure facing away in the corner. A trail of footprints leading toward a wall. Say nothing about them. See what the player does.

### Crossing into Tier 2 (Sanity 49)

This should feel like the world has decided to be honest with something it was hiding. Layer 2 is the tier where geography changes — where a wall becomes a door, where a dead end becomes a passage. The first Layer 2 crossing should involve a spatial revelation: the Weeping Fresco entrance, a hidden chamber in the Whispering Caves, a staircase that wasn't there.

**DM action at this threshold:** Place Layer 2 cutouts with more deliberateness than Layer 1. The grotto entrance in the Salty Squid should feel like a mouth opening in the wall. The hidden passage in the caves should feel like the mountain exhaling a secret it had been holding. Frame the spatial revelation before placing the cutout — describe the subtle wrongness the character notices first — then place the piece as the moment of full recognition.

### Crossing into Tier 3 (Sanity 24)

This should feel like a reckoning. Tier 3 is where the truth of the world becomes fully visible, and the truth is the petrified creature, the dead god's heartbeat, the cult's amplification plan, the Shine as wound rather than weather. This tier should not arrive gently.

**DM action at this threshold:** The Layer 3 revelation in the Whispering Caves (the fossilised anatomy visible throughout the cave system, the inner ear chamber, the still-beating heart) is the designed delivery mechanism for this milestone. If possible, time the Tier 3 crossing to occur during the descent into the deeper Whispering Caves — the revelation and the mechanical threshold should land simultaneously. Place all Layer 3 elements at once rather than gradually. The effect should be overwhelming.

### Approaching Tier 4 (Sanity 1–10)

Characters in this range should be visibly, mechanically, behaviourally different from their companions. The Tier 3 disadvantage on Charisma checks with Tier 0 NPCs should be generating roleplay friction. The character should be saying things that sound like prophecy to their companions and like obvious observation to themselves.

**DM action in this range:** Begin foreshadowing the Tier 4 conversion possibility without triggering it. The Silent Oracle encounter, if available, is the designed mechanism for this — a vision of what complete Understanding looks like, from the outside, should make the Tier 3 character understand what they are approaching. Give them the chance to pull back, and also give them the chance to decide it doesn't matter.

---

## The Restoration Gradient: How Much Is Too Much?

Sanity restoration is available, intentional, and necessary. Without it, the campaign's Sanity gradient collapses — every character races toward Tier 4 and the game stops being about the cost of understanding and starts being about survival arithmetic.

The restoration system is designed to create a gradient that oscillates within a general downward trend. Characters should feel Sanity loss as a resource being spent rather than a countdown to failure. The oscillation — losing some, recovering some, losing more — is what makes individual thresholds meaningful.

**Healthy oscillation:** A character loses 15 Sanity in a tough encounter, recovers 8 from a grounding activity and an NPC conversation, then loses 12 in the next major horror event. Their net movement is downward but punctuated, and each individual loss and recovery feels like a decision or an event rather than background radiation.

**Unhealthy flat loss:** A character loses 3–5 Sanity per scene consistently with no recovery, producing a slow grind that makes the final 30 points feel identical to the first 30. This is the most common failure mode and it is usually the result of under-using restoration opportunities rather than over-using Sanity loss triggers.

**Unhealthy over-recovery:** A party that aggressively farms restoration methods (buying every Glimmer Bloom Vial available, using the mountain spring every long rest, confiding in NPCs after every session) can maintain Sanity above 70 indefinitely. If this happens, apply gentle environmental pressure: reduce Glimmer Bloom availability as the blight spreads, have the mountain spring become inaccessible due to a cult patrol, make NPCs less available as the threat escalates. The world becoming more hostile should make restoration harder as well as Sanity loss more frequent.

**Target oscillation range by act:**

| Act | Healthy Range | Recovery Frequency |
|---|---|---|
| Act I | 70–100 | Once per 2 sessions |
| Act II (early) | 50–75 | Once per session |
| Act II (late) | 35–60 | Twice per session |
| Act III | 15–45 | Available but difficult |

---

## The Whispering Mender as Pacing Tool

The Whispering Mender (creature 5) is not just an enemy — it is a DM pacing instrument. Its Soothing Whisper ability can push a character above a tier threshold, ejecting them from layer-visible content. Used deliberately, this gives the DM fine-grained control over which characters have access to which tiers at any given moment.

**Using the Mender to slow a party moving too fast:** Station a Mender near a high-Understanding-layer encounter. If the party's Tier 3 character is about to access content before the story is ready for it, the Mender targets them first. A successful Soothing Whisper pushes them back to Tier 2. The layer content disappears from their perception. The story has bought itself a session.

**Using the Mender to teach a plateaued party:** If a party has been sitting at Tier 1 for too long and seems comfortable there, introduce a Mender encounter. Watching the Mender try to heal a character out of their tier — and understanding that the creature is trying to take something from them — generates a relationship to tier access that pure Sanity loss cannot create. After a Mender encounter, players often actively resist restoration for the first time.

---

## A Quick Reference: Campaign Milestones and Their Sanity Context

| Milestone | Expected Party Sanity | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Arrival in Stiltsmere | 100 | Full consensus reality |
| First Soursight encounter | 85–95 | First save, first loss |
| Crime scene investigation | 75–90 | Tier 1 crossing likely here |
| Shattering Event | **49 (fixed)** | Engineered threshold crossing |
| Weeping Fresco attempt | 49–65 | Ejection expected; tutorial complete |
| Isle of Reeds submerged shrine | 45–65 | Tier 2 stable |
| Whispering Caves entrance | 40–60 | |
| Ossian confrontation | 30–55 | |
| Petrified Heart (Layer 3 revelation) | 20–45 | Tier 3 crossing ideally here |
| The City in Mercury (if pursued) | 15–35 | High Understanding required |
| Echo of the Maw Phase 1 | 10–30 | Tier 2 required to perceive anchors |
| Echo of the Maw Phase 2 | 5–25 | Final push |

---

*"Sanity & Scaling Guide" is a companion document to "The Souring of Stiltsmere: A Campaign Guide." It is intended for DM use only and should not be shared with players.*

---

# The Souring of Stiltsmere: A Campaign Guide
### *Table-Ready Fixed Edition*

---

## **Part 0: Session Zero Guide**

*Read this section before running the first session. Share relevant portions with your players.*

### What Kind of Campaign This Is

"The Souring of Stiltsmere" is a **cosmic horror mystery campaign** that will ask more of your players than most D&D adventures. It is not a campaign about being a hero. It is a campaign about being a witness. Characters will make meaningful discoveries, take meaningful actions, and potentially save the world — but they will be changed irrevocably in the process. The campaign's central premise is that understanding the truth costs something, and that cost is structural, mechanical, and unavoidable.

This is the right campaign for groups who enjoy:

- **Investigation and deduction** over combat as the primary problem-solving mode
- **Atmospheric horror and psychological dread** rather than jump-scare gore
- **Mechanical surprises** — systems that subvert standard D&D assumptions
- **Moral ambiguity** — the antagonists have coherent, even sympathetic worldviews
- **Bittersweet or complex endings** over unambiguous triumph

### Content Discussion: What to Address Before Session One

Before play begins, have an honest conversation with your players about the following elements.

**Sanity as a Mechanical Resource.** This campaign uses a custom Sanity/Understanding system that will be explained at the first session. Sanity is not a hit point bar that depletes toward failure — losing Sanity unlocks new content, new perceptions, and new capabilities. However, reaching 0 Sanity results in a form of **character conversion**: the character becomes an NPC under the DM's control. This is not arbitrary, and the character can continue to exist as a meaningful story presence, but it functions as a kind of character death. Players should know this possibility exists before it happens.

**The Reality-Devouring Gaze.** The campaign's final boss possesses an ability that can **permanently erase a character from existence**, leaving no body and requiring a *Wish* spell to restore them. This is the appropriate dramatic stakes for a cosmic horror climax, but no player should encounter this as a surprise. The DM is encouraged to foreshadow this possibility through in-world means — visions, NPC warnings, lore from The City in Mercury — at least one session before the final confrontation, and may optionally disclose it as a general campaign feature at Session Zero.

**Body Horror and Psychological Distress.** The campaign contains descriptions of mutilated bodies, corrupted wildlife, fragmented identities, and experiences of unreality. Use the X-card or Lines and Veils framework if your table uses safety tools, and establish any hard limits on graphic description before play begins.

**The Murder Investigation Framing.** The campaign begins as a murder mystery. Players should know that this framing is intentional misdirection — the scope will expand dramatically after Act I. If any of your players strongly prefer self-contained mystery adventures, this campaign may not be the right fit.

### Character Creation Guidance

**Recommended Backgrounds:** Sage, Investigator, Outlander, Sailor, Folk Hero, Criminal, and Spy all have natural narrative hooks for the investigation-heavy early acts.

**Regarding Wisdom:** Classes with high Wisdom saves will resist Sanity loss more reliably — a double-edged advantage. Characters with lower Wisdom saves may descend into higher Understanding Tiers more quickly, granting earlier access to layered content at the cost of greater instability. Both experiences are valid and intended.

**Starting Sanity:** All characters begin with **100 Sanity** regardless of class or ability scores. Wisdom modifiers affect saving throws against Sanity loss, not the starting value.

### The DM's Core Commitments

Running this campaign well requires two principles:

1. **The layers are real.** When a character gains Understanding, they are genuinely perceiving something that is there. Hidden-layer content is objective fact, not hallucination. The horror comes from the content, not from gaslighting players.

2. **Madness is not failure.** Sanity loss should be framed neutrally, never punitively. When a character descends into a higher Understanding Tier, they are not losing — they are transforming.

---
## **Part I: Introduction & Core Concepts**

### Campaign Overview

"The Souring of Stiltsmere" is a cosmic horror campaign for a group of 3–5 players, designed to span multiple sessions and character levels. It begins as a seemingly straightforward murder investigation on a remote, fog-shrouded archipelago and gradually unravels into a desperate struggle against a primordial entity seeking to break through the fabric of reality. The campaign heavily utilizes a unique **Sanity/Understanding** mechanic, where a character's diminishing grip on reality paradoxically grants them a deeper insight into the horrifying truths of the world, revealing new layers of the game world itself.

The tone is one of creeping dread, psychological horror, and existential mystery. Combat is often dangerous and less a solution than a consequence. Investigation, social interaction, and careful management of one's own mind are paramount to survival. The players are not heroes destined for glory; they are desperate individuals caught in a tide of cosmic indifference, whose greatest victory might be to simply endure.

---

### Terminology & Vocabulary Index

- **Stiltsmere:** The name of the archipelago and its main port town. It consists of three primary islands connected by bridges, surrounded by the Lake of Tears.
- **The Lake of Tears:** The vast, unnaturally deep lake that formed in a single night, creating the Stiltsmere archipelago. Its waters are cold, dark, and eerily still.
- **The Shattering Shine:** The permanent, mirror-like fog that blankets the Lake of Tears and often creeps onto the shores of the islands. It reflects everything with perfect clarity, but the reflections are subtly *wrong*.
- **Soursights:** A profoundly disturbing phenomenon occurring within the Shattering Shine. When enveloped by the fog, a person sees reflections of themselves, their companions, and their surroundings. These reflections, however, are all staring directly at the viewer with wide, silent, open mouths. The experience is accompanied by a cloying, sour smell. Witnessing a Soursight is a direct assault on one's sanity.
- **Sanity:** A quantifiable measure of a character's mental fortitude and connection to consensus reality. It starts at 100 and decreases through exposure to horrific events, creatures, and knowledge.
- **Understanding:** The inverse of Sanity. It begins at 0 and increases as Sanity is lost. Understanding represents a character's attunement to the hidden, maddening truths of the cosmos. It is divided into five tiers, each unlocking a new "layer" of perception.
- **Layers:** Translucent overlays the DM places on the game map, representing what characters with sufficient Understanding can perceive. These can add new paths, clues, creatures, and even entire locations that are invisible and intangible to those with low Understanding.
- **The Souring:** The name given by the cult to the final event they wish to bring about — the complete merging of the reflected reality within the Shattering Shine with the physical world, allowing their patron deity to manifest.
- **The One Who Watches from Silver:** The primordial, non-Euclidean consciousness that exists within the reflections of the Shattering Shine. It is not a god to be worshipped in a traditional sense, but a force of nature to be channeled. It is the source of the Soursights and the entity the cult serves.
- **Murk-folk:** The common name for the inhabitants of the central port island, the Isle of Murk.
- **Crag-herders:** The hardy, stoic people of the southern mountainous island, the Isle of Crags.
- **Reed-walkers:** The reclusive, stilt-farming communities of the northern island, the Isle of Reeds.
- **The City in Mercury:** The true, ancient name of the submerged city whose peaks form a hidden fourth island. Its existence is a secret known only to those with profound Understanding or who have uncovered the deepest lore.

---

### Player Packet: An Introduction to Stiltsmere

*(This should be given to the players before session one.)*

Welcome to Stiltsmere.

You have been hired as a team of investigators, bounty hunters, or perhaps simply capable individuals, brought together by a lucrative contract. A gruesome murder has shaken the insular community of Stiltsmere, a remote archipelago perpetually shrouded in a bizarre, reflective fog. Your journey will take you across a cold, deep lake to this isolated chain of islands, a place with its own strange customs and deep-seated secrets.

Your contact, a woman named Elara, represents a powerful local family and has promised a handsome reward for identifying the killer of a cannery worker named Finnian. The local watch is inept and superstitious, making outside help a necessity.

Be warned: Stiltsmere is not like other places. The fog, which the locals call the "Shattering Shine," plays tricks on the eyes and the mind. The people are wary of outsiders, and the islands themselves seem to hold an ancient, slumbering resentment.

**Things to Know:**

- **The Setting:** An isolated archipelago of three islands. Travel between them is by a series of long, weathered bridges.
- **The Fog:** The Shattering Shine is a constant presence. It can be unnervingly beautiful and dangerously disorienting. Locals have a healthy fear of it, especially when it rolls in thick from the lake.
- **Your Goal:** Solve the murder of Finnian. Report your findings to Elara. Collect your pay. It sounds simple enough.
- **Mental Fortitude:** The events you may witness in Stiltsmere can be taxing on the mind. Keep track of your character's mental state. Some horrors leave scars that cannot be seen. Your grip on what is real may be tested.

Prepare yourselves. The boat to Stiltsmere leaves at dusk.

---
## **Part II: The World of Stiltsmere**

### The Opening Vignette & The Fisherman's Song

The campaign begins in the final hour of a long, unsettling boat ride. The Player Characters are crammed into a small, sturdy fishing skiff piloted by an old, grizzled fisherman named Silas. He is one of the few who can reliably navigate the Shattering Shine. The air is cold and smells of brine, wet wood, and something faintly sour. All around them is the fog, a roiling wall of silver that perfectly mirrors the boat, the churning water, and the faces of the PCs. The only sound is the creak of the hull and Silas's low, gravelly voice as he sings.

> *(DM's Narration)*
> "The air grows colder as Silas's small boat cuts through the unnaturally calm water of the Lake of Tears. All around you, the Shattering Shine closes in — a silent, silver wall reflecting a perfect, yet unnerving, duplicate of your vessel. In the reflection, you see yourselves, but your mirrored eyes seem to hold a different light, a vacant curiosity. The old fisherman, Silas, his face a roadmap of wrinkles, pays the reflections no mind. He grips the tiller with one hand and, in a voice like grinding stones, begins to sing an old, mournful tune."

**The Song of the Fracture:**

*(To be sung slowly, in a low folk-ballad style)*

> "The ground did groan, the stone did weep,
> While old gods slumbered in the deep.
> The mountain's spine, it bent and broke,
> A word of ruin, softly spoke.
>
> The peaks let loose a silver tear,
> To drown the vale and quell the fear.
> The basin filled in one dark night,
> And stole away the morning's light.
>
> What's left is three, where many stood,
> Three lonely isles of rock and wood.
> And on the water, cold and wide,
> A silver fog, where reflections hide.
>
> So watch the Shine, when it drifts near,
> And see yourself, consumed by fear.
> For in the glass, a mouth gapes wide,
> A hunger born on sorrow's tide."

As Silas finishes the last verse, the prow of the boat bumps gently against a wooden dock. Through a thinning patch of fog, the dim, flickering lanterns of Stiltsmere's port town can be seen. "We're here," Silas grunts, tying off the rope. "Don't stare too long at your own face in the Shine. It has a way of starin' back."

---

### Detailed Island Descriptions

#### 1. The Isle of Murk (The Middle Island)

This is the heart of the archipelago, home to the main port town, also called Stiltsmere. It is the hub of trade, governance, and social life.

- **Topography & Environment:** The most geologically stable of the three islands. It's relatively flat, with muddy shores and a few low, rolling hills inland. The air is thick with the smell of fish, salt, damp earth, and coal smoke from the cannery. The architecture is a hodgepodge of weathered wood and stone, with buildings leaning against each other for support. The ever-present fog often reduces visibility in the narrow, winding streets to mere feet.
- **Culture & People (The Murk-folk):** The Murk-folk are pragmatic, superstitious, and deeply distrustful of outsiders. They are a mix of fishermen, cannery workers, merchants, and the families that run the town. They speak in clipped sentences and avoid eye contact. They have a complex relationship with the Shine — it provides the isolation that protects their community, but they fear the Soursights and the strange things sometimes seen moving within the fog.
- **Key Locations:**
  - **The Docks:** Where the PCs arrive. A bustling, chaotic area of fishing boats, cargo crates, and the constant shouts of workers.
  - **The Gaping Maw Cannery:** The largest employer on the islands and the scene of the initial murder. A hulking, soot-stained building that chugs out black smoke day and night. The smell of cooked fish and ozone is overpowering.
  - **The Salty Squid Inn:** The only public house that tolerates outsiders. A dimly lit, grimy place run by a stern, one-eyed woman named Brenna. This will serve as the PCs' base of operations.
  - **The Tide-Worn Trinkets:** A cluttered shop run by an eccentric old man named Master Corwin, who deals in salvage, antiques, and local lore. He is a valuable, if cryptic, source of information.
  - **The Harbormaster's Office:** A small, official-looking building where the island's nominal law, Harbormaster Greaves, resides. He is overworked, under-equipped, and wants this murder solved with minimal fuss.
  - **The Blackwood Estate:** The large, foreboding home of the wealthiest family on the islands, perched on a hill overlooking the town. The PCs' initial quest-giver, Elara, is an associate of this family.

- **Layered Map Design Example: The Salty Squid Inn**
  - **Base Layer (Understanding 0):** A typical, dreary inn. A bar, several tables, a hearth, and a few glum patrons nursing their drinks. The air is smoky. Brenna the innkeeper cleans glasses behind the bar. There is a large, mundane painting of a stormy sea above the hearth.
  - **Understanding Layer 1 (Sanity 74–50):** The patrons sometimes flicker, and for a split second, their mouths are wide open, staring at the PC who can perceive this layer. The painting of the sea now seems to *move* — the waves churn with subtle, unnatural slowness. Faint, wet footprints can sometimes be seen appearing and fading on the floorboards, leading from the door to an empty corner. Whispers can be heard in the crackling of the fire.
  - **Understanding Layer 2 (Sanity 49–25):** The ghostly patrons are more solid, their silent, gaping mouths a constant presence. One of them, a spectral fisherman, sits at a table and will beckon a PC over if they are noticed. He cannot speak but will point a trembling, translucent finger towards the painting. The painting itself now clearly depicts a strange, non-Euclidean city beneath the waves. The wet footprints now lead to a section of wall in the corner. Interacting with this wall reveals a hidden, damp crawlspace that smells of the deep ocean — a small, 2-room dungeon (The Weeping Fresco) that is not physically there on the base layer.

#### 2. The Isle of Crags (The Southern Island)

A treacherous, mountainous island connected to the Isle of Murk by a long, high-arched stone bridge. It is sparsely populated and considered dangerous by most islanders.

- **Topography & Environment:** Dominated by sharp, granite peaks that tear at the fog, creating strange drafts and clearings. The terrain is difficult, consisting of scree slopes, narrow ledges, and deep chasms. Cave systems riddle the mountains. The air is thin, cold, and carries the sound of wind whistling through the rocks.
- **Culture & People (The Crag-herders):** A hardy, stoic people who live in small, isolated communities. They raise a unique breed of sure-footed, three-legged mountain goat called a "Tripod." They are deeply spiritual, worshipping the mountain spirits and having a particular reverence for silence. They are masters of traversal and survival in their harsh environment.
- **The Tripod Goats:** These creatures are a biological marvel, with a third leg extending from their sternum that acts as a natural piolet, allowing them to brace themselves on sheer cliffs. Their milk is thick and nutritious, and their horns are used to craft tools.
- **Mechanic: Walking Picks:** The Crag-herders craft these tools from Tripod horns. They function like advanced climbing axes, with a weighted pick on one end and a broad, shovel-like head on the other. A character proficient with Walking Picks can treat all rocky, mountainous terrain as normal terrain and gain advantage on checks to avoid falling or losing their footing on the Isle of Crags.
- **Key Locations:**
  - **High-Pass Bridge:** The only connection to the Isle of Murk. It is ancient and often slick with mist.
  - **Shepherd's Rest:** The largest (and only) village on the island, nestled in a high-altitude valley. It is here the PCs can interact with the Crag-herders and potentially earn a pair of Walking Picks by completing a quest.
  - **The Whispering Caves:** A network of tunnels known for strange acoustic properties. The wind blowing through them is said to sound like voices. This is a primary location for a cult hideout.
  - **The Ancient Lighthouse:** Perched on the southernmost, most treacherous peak. It is derelict and hasn't been lit in centuries. It holds the key to revealing the sunken city of The City in Mercury.
- **Quest Hook: The Lost Herd:** A respected Crag-herder elder, Kaelen, has lost a prize Tripod goat to a deep chasm. He offers the PCs a set of masterfully crafted Walking Picks if they can brave the chasm and retrieve the animal, or at least discover its fate. This leads them into a part of the Whispering Caves system, where they might find the goat... and early signs of the cult's presence.

#### 3. The Isle of Reeds (The Northern Island)

A low-lying, marshy island connected by a long, rickety wooden causeway. It is known for its strange agriculture and reclusive inhabitants.

- **Topography & Environment:** Almost entirely composed of flooded fields, bogs, and dense reed beds. The ground is soft and unstable. Patches of the Shattering Shine form at ground level here more than anywhere else, making travel on foot perilous. The air is heavy, humid, and smells of wet earth, decaying vegetation, and the faint, sweet scent of the local crop.
- **Culture & People (The Reed-walkers):** The Reed-walkers are a tall, slender people who have adapted to their environment by living and working on stilts. They are isolationist and deeply suspicious of the Murk-folk, who they see as loud and impure. Their entire culture revolves around the cultivation of the "Glimmer Bloom," a bioluminescent, chalk-white plant that grows underwater and resembles a cross between a lotus and lettuce. They also harvest the bitter "Shinefruit" from sickly-looking palm-like trees to distill a potent, hallucinogenic liquor.
- **Mechanic: Farming Stilts:** These are four-limbed contraptions. Two long stilts attach to the legs, and two shorter, articulated stilts are held in the arms, tipped with farming implements. A character can learn to use a modified set for travel. Using the stilts allows a character to move through the flooded fields at a normal pace and elevates them above most ground-level fog, granting them advantage on saving throws against Soursights that originate from these low-lying patches. Mastering the stilts requires a skill check (Dexterity-based).
- **Key Locations:**
  - **The Rickety Causeway:** The long, swaying wooden bridge connecting to the Isle of Murk. It is poorly maintained and parts of it are often submerged during high water.
  - **Stilt-Town:** The main settlement, a collection of huts and platforms built on stilts high above the water. It is connected by a network of rope bridges and narrow planks.
  - **The Glimmer Fields:** The vast, flooded plains where the Glimmer Blooms are cultivated. At night, the fields give off a soft, eerie white light.
  - **The Grove of Bitter Palms:** The location where the sickly Shinefruit trees grow. The area is known to have a high concentration of Soursights.
- **Quest Hook: The Blighted Bloom:** The Glimmer Blooms in one of the fields have started to turn black and rot. The Reed-walkers believe it to be a curse. They are willing to teach the PCs the Way of the Stilt and reward them handsomely if they can venture into the Blighted Field and cleanse it of its corruption. The source is a submerged cult artifact poisoning the water, guarded by fog-spawned creatures.

#### 4. The City in Mercury (The Sunken City)

This is not a true island but the highest peaks of a vast, cyclopean city that was submerged when the Lake of Tears formed. It is a completely optional, high-risk, high-reward endgame location.

- **Access:** It can only be revealed by a character with **Understanding 3 (Sanity 24 or lower)**. They must first get the quest from a reflection of themselves in the Salty Squid Inn. This reflection, visible only to them, will silently hand them a complex celestial chart. This chart, when taken to the Ancient Lighthouse on the Isle of Crags and used to align its long-defunct mechanism, will cause the lighthouse to project a beam of pure, shadowless light. This light does not illuminate but *erases* a patch of the Shattering Shine, revealing the jagged, obsidian peaks of The City in Mercury. A pathway of solidified fog forms, allowing passage.
- **Environment:** The architecture is non-Euclidean and deeply unsettling. The air within is dry, sterile, and silent. There is no natural life. The city is a labyrinth of impossible angles, towering monoliths, and vast, empty plazas. Gravity and perception can be warped in certain areas.
- **Lore & Rewards:** The City in Mercury was the city of the entity's original servants, a pre-human race that triggered the Fracture in a failed ritual. The city is a treasure trove of profound lore, powerful but sanity-blasting artifacts, and answers to the ultimate questions of the campaign.
  - **Reward Example 1: The Amulet of Still Reflections.** A silver amulet that allows the wearer, once per day, to become perfectly still and be treated as invisible by any creature spawned from the Shattering Shine.
  - **Reward Example 2: The Tome of the Unseen Visage.** Reading this tome grants an immediate and permanent +1 to the reader's Understanding Tier (e.g., a character at Tier 1 moves to Tier 2) but permanently reduces their maximum Sanity by 20.
  - **Reward Example 3: The Crystalline Resonator.** A fist-sized, humming crystal. When struck, it emits a tone that forces all creatures within 30 feet to make a Sanity saving throw. Creatures of the Shine have disadvantage. However, the user must also make a saving throw at a lower DC.

---
## **Part III: Mechanics & Systems**

### The Sanity & Understanding System

This is the core mechanic of the campaign. Every character starts with **100 Sanity** and **0 Understanding**.

- **Sanity Saving Throws:** Unless a creature, hazard, item, or scene specifies otherwise, a Sanity saving throw is a **Wisdom saving throw**. The DC is set by the scene or effect.
- **Losing Sanity:** Characters make Sanity saving throws when they encounter Soursights, witness grotesque events, face horrifying creatures, or learn forbidden lore. On a failed save, they lose the listed amount of Sanity. Minor shocks usually cost **1d4–1d6 Sanity**; severe revelations or direct contact with the Shine usually cost **2d8–3d10 Sanity**.
- **Gaining Understanding:** As Sanity drops, Understanding rises through five tiers. The DM should inform a player when their character crosses a threshold.
- **Cumulative Layer Access:** Layer access is cumulative. A Tier 2 character sees and interacts with **Layer 1 and Layer 2** content. A Tier 3 character sees and interacts with **Layers 1, 2, and 3**.
- **Threshold Precision:** Thresholds are exact. **50 Sanity is still Tier 1. 49 Sanity begins Tier 2. 25 Sanity is still Tier 2. 24 Sanity begins Tier 3. 1 Sanity is still Tier 3. 0 Sanity begins Tier 4.**
- **Threshold Disorientation:** When a character involuntarily drops into a new Understanding Tier because of Sanity loss, they must succeed on a **DC 12 Constitution saving throw** or become **disoriented** until the end of their next turn. A disoriented creature has disadvantage on its next attack roll, ability check, or saving throw. Voluntary, controlled threshold-crossing effects such as **Shinefruit Concentrate** can bypass this penalty if they say so.
- **Temporary or Permanent Understanding Modifiers:** Some rare items and revelations can grant Understanding independent of current Sanity. These effects create a **minimum effective Understanding Tier** for perception and puzzle access, but they do not automatically lower current Sanity unless the item says so. If restoration raises the character above their normal threshold, the item-granted minimum still applies.

**The Five Tiers of Understanding:**

**Tier 0: The Veil (Sanity 100–75 | Understanding 0)**
- **Perception:** The world is as it seems. The character is grounded in normal reality.
- **Mechanics:** No special abilities or penalties. They are unable to perceive anything on higher Understanding layers.

**Tier 1: The Crack (Sanity 74–50 | Understanding 1)**
- **Perception:** The character begins to notice inconsistencies. A flicker in the corner of the eye, a whisper on the wind, a feeling of being watched. They can see the subtle changes of the first layer.
- **Mechanics:** Can perceive and interact with elements on **Layer 1**. They may see hidden clues, faint sigils, impossible footprints, or minor non-hostile apparitions. They gain advantage on checks made specifically to detect supernatural ambushes or Soursight pressure.
- **Drawback:** They are susceptible to effects and creatures that exist on Layer 1. Mundane details may begin to look symbolically loaded, though the DM should favour unsettling true information over arbitrary falsehoods.

**Tier 2: The Glimpse (Sanity 49–25 | Understanding 2)**
- **Perception:** The world begins to actively warp. Hidden paths, secret doors, and entirely new routes become visible. Horrific creatures that were previously invisible are now plain to see.
- **Mechanics:** Can perceive and interact with **Layer 1 and Layer 2** content. This is where new dungeon entrances, hidden NPCs, hostile spectral enemies, and altered geography become playable.
- **Drawback:** The line between real and unreal blurs. When presenting misleading perception, the DM should twist true information rather than simply lie. A real doorway might look wet with fire. A harmless echo might look predatory. A safe path might appear like a wound.

**Tier 3: The Knowing (Sanity 24–1 | Understanding 3)**
- **Perception:** The character sees the base reality and the hidden reality superimposed on each other. They see the cosmic machinery at work. They understand the patterns inside the madness.
- **Mechanics:** Can perceive and interact with **Layers 1, 2, and 3**. They can see reality-strings, ritual networks, impossible geometry, and puzzle solutions that lower-tier characters cannot meaningfully parse. Tier 3 characters can receive the quest-hook toward The City in Mercury.
- **Drawback:** Their grasp on social norms and ordinary causality is frayed. They suffer disadvantage on Charisma-based checks with ordinary Tier 0 people unless those people already trust them. They may struggle to distinguish an immediate threat from a symbolic or prophetic one.

**Tier 4: The Unraveling (Sanity 0 | Understanding 4 — The Final Tier)**
- **Perception:** The character no longer sees consensus reality. They see only the screaming, kaleidoscopic truth of the cosmos. They are lost to the Souring.
- **Mechanics:** The character becomes an NPC under the DM's control. They are not necessarily hostile, but their goals are alien and incomprehensible. They may become a tragic oracle, a monstrous husk, or a willing servant of The One Who Watches from Silver. This is effectively character death, but with the potential for the character to return as a recurring, story-relevant NPC.

---

### The Layered Map System (Practical Implementation)

1. **Preparation:** The DM prepares normal base maps of key locations: the Cannery, the Salty Squid, the Whispering Caves, the Blackwood Estate, and any ritual sites used in the finale.
2. **Base Map:** This is the physical, tangible location that everyone at Tier 0 can see.
3. **Modular Layer Elements:** Do **not** prepare three complete alternate maps for every location. Prepare small reusable transparent cutouts, tokens, or VTT objects: ghost figures, footprint strips, sigils, hidden doors, spectral creature tokens, ritual circles, anatomy overlays, and reality-fracture lines.
4. **Tier Use:** When a character reaches a new Understanding Tier, place the elements for that tier where the scene calls for them. Because layer access is cumulative, a Tier 3 character sees the elements from Layers 1, 2, and 3.
   - **Layer 1:** Faint bloodstains, glowing sigils, ghostly silhouettes, wet footprints, wrong shadows.
   - **Layer 2:** Hidden doors, new paths, chasms, ritual circles, hostile spectral creatures, Soursight faces.
   - **Layer 3:** Ritual networks, reality-strings, impossible geometry, complete anatomical revelation, true ritual vulnerabilities.
5. **Shared Table Visibility:** All players may see the layer elements out of character, but only characters with sufficient Understanding can act on them in character. This creates dramatic irony instead of bookkeeping friction. If your table struggles with that separation, use private VTT visibility or give the relevant player a note.
6. **Restoration and Removal:** If Sanity restoration moves a character back above a threshold, remove only the content they can no longer perceive. Example: restoring from 48 to 50 removes Layer 2 access but preserves Layer 1 access. Healing can therefore feel both relieving and frightening, because it takes information away.

---
## **Part IV: The Campaign**

### Main Campaign Guide

**Act I: The Murder at the Gaping Maw (Sessions 1–3)**

- **The Hook:** The PCs arrive, meet their contact Elara Vane, a poised and concerned representative of the Blackwood family. She explains that a cannery worker, Finnian, was found brutally murdered inside the cannery's processing room. The body was... arranged. She provides them with an advance and lodging at the Salty Squid.

- **The Investigation:**
  - **The Crime Scene:** The cannery is a maze of machinery and steam. Finnian's body was found splayed on a cutting table, his mouth wide open, and his eyes removed. A strange, spiral symbol is painted on the floor in a mixture of fish guts and his own blood. A character with **Understanding 1** can see the symbol faintly glowing.
  - **Clues:** A fellow worker saw Finnian arguing with a quiet, reclusive man named Malakai. Finnian's small shack contains ramblings and drawings of Soursights and the same spiral symbol. A piece of dark, ritualistic cloth was snagged on a machine near the body.
  - **The Cannery Office — A Critical Discovery (Subplot A Node):** A thorough search of the cannery office (requiring a **DC 14 Intelligence (Investigation)** check) uncovers a hidden compartment behind a loose wall panel near the floor safe. Inside is a shipping ledger bearing the Blackwood family crest. The ledger is not concealed out of incompetence — it has been hidden because it documents the Blackwood family's shipments of unusual alchemical reagents (quicksilver, nightshade, powdered obsidian) to "private storage" on the Isle of Crags, dating back years. *This is the entry point for Subplot A: The Blackwood Family Secret.* Players who find the ledger can take it to Master Corwin, who will identify the reagents and nervously suggest "those materials don't go into cannery work, and they don't come from any reputable merchant." Presenting the ledger to Elara in a private confrontation will trigger the full Subplot A revelation (see Part: Events, Quests, and Secrets, Subplot A). The DM should ensure this discovery opportunity is visible early, as it transforms Elara from a simple quest-giver into one of the campaign's most complex moral figures.
  - **The Trail:** Questioning the townsfolk reveals Malakai is a known recluse who lives near the shore. Investigating his hovel reveals more cult paraphernalia and a journal detailing his induction into a group that "welcomes the Sights." He writes about a "purification" ritual that went wrong. Malakai is missing.

- **Resolution:** The PCs track Malakai to a seaside cave (a small, introductory dungeon). He is in the middle of a ritual, attempting to "offer" another kidnapped cannery worker to the Shine. Malakai is the murderer, but he is a terrified, low-ranking cultist, not a master planner. He reveals he was ordered to kill Finnian because Finnian was losing his nerve and planned to expose the cult. He should reveal the **shape** of his superiors rather than simply naming Greaves: *"The man from the harbor office. The one with clean gloves. He never gave me his name inside the circle."* Upon being defeated or captured, Malakai warns them that this is just the beginning and that "The Souring is coming." The PCs have solved the murder, but have uncovered a far greater conspiracy. Elara pays them for the murder, but, shaken by the cult's implications, offers them a new and more dangerous contract to eliminate the threat to Stiltsmere.

- **Optional Consequence — The Cannery Horror:** By default, Act I climaxes with Malakai and the attempted sacrifice. The **Cannery Horror** is not the required Act I boss. Use it only if Malakai completes his ritual, if the party ignores the cannery after the murder, or if you want the finale to foreshadow the cult's attempt to build a physical vessel. Otherwise, keep the Horror dormant until Act III as a failed prototype or environmental guardian inside the Gaping Maw.

---

**Act II: The Whispering Cult (Sessions 4–8)**

- **The Goal:** Identify the cult's leadership and stop their plan. The investigation will lead them across all three islands.
- **The Isle of Reeds:** The strange cloth from the crime scene is identified by Master Corwin as being woven from the Blighted Glimmer Blooms of the northern isle. The PCs travel there, deal with the Blighted Field quest, and discover a submerged shrine where the cult performs baptisms in the tainted water to "open the mind."
- **The Isle of Crags:** Malakai's journal mentions meetings in the "place where the mountain whispers." This leads the PCs to the Whispering Caves on the southern island. Here they can undertake the Lost Herd quest to gain Walking Picks, making traversal easier.
- **The Cult Hideout:** Deep within the Whispering Caves is the cult's main base of operations. This is a major dungeon crawl. Here, the layered map system comes into full play.
  - **Layer 1** reveals the cult's true path through the caves.
  - **Layer 2** reveals their horrific ritual chambers and the spectral guardians bound to them.
  - **Layer 3** might reveal that the cave system is part of the petrified anatomy of a massive, long-dead creature.
- **The Leadership:** The cult is led by a triumvirate: a charismatic Crag-herder shaman (The Voice of the Mountain) who interprets the "whispers" of the caves, a manipulative Reed-walker who brews potions from the Shinefruit to induce visions (The Tender of the Bloom), and a seemingly respectable member of Stiltsmere's elite — **Harbormaster Greaves**. Greaves has been using his position to cover the cult's tracks and acquire resources.
- **The Plan:** The PCs uncover the cult's full plan: they are preparing for a grand ritual during an upcoming celestial alignment. They will sacrifice dozens of islanders at three focal points — one on each island — to create a massive Soursight, a "wound" in reality, through which The One Who Watches from Silver can manifest.

---

**Act III: The Souring (Sessions 9+)**

Act III is the campaign's pressure-cooker. The mystery phase has ended. The party knows enough to act, but not enough to act without risk. The cult's plan is distributed across three island focal points. Each ritual site can be disrupted independently, and the number of disrupted sites determines the campaign ending.

### Act III Pressure Clock

Use this clock once the party discovers the full Souring plan in the Whispering Caves, Blackwood Estate, or Greaves's documents. Adjust exact timing to your table's pacing, but preserve the feeling that every detour costs something.

| Time Remaining | Cult Action | World Change | DM Use |
|---|---|---|---|
| **6 days** | Captives are identified and marked. Supply caches move to the three ritual sites. | Fog lingers longer after dawn. Reflections lag by one heartbeat. | Give the party freedom to plan. |
| **5 days** | The Reeds site begins poisoning the Glimmer Bloom root network. | Glimmer Fields pulse unevenly; Reed-walkers report nausea and strange dreams. | Push the Reeds hook. |
| **4 days** | The Crags device begins test amplification. | The mountain whispers become audible to Tier 0 characters as almost-words. | Push the Crags hook. |
| **3 days** | The Murk captives are moved through Greaves's harbor routes. | Soursights appear in ordinary puddles and kitchen knives. | Make Greaves feel active. |
| **2 days** | The three focal sites synchronize. | Random Fog Events become harsher; add +2 to Sanity save DCs caused by fog. | Encourage commitment. |
| **1 day** | Final sacrifices are prepared. | The Shine appears in daylight. Mouths open in every reflection. | No more safe errands. |
| **Alignment night** | The rituals begin. | The three islands act as one ritual diagram around the Lake of Tears. | Run the finale. |

### The Three Ritual Sites

Each ritual site has four moving parts:

1. **A visible problem** the local population understands imperfectly.
2. **A cult engine** performing the actual metaphysical work.
3. **A layer-hidden vulnerability** that low-Understanding characters cannot perceive.
4. **A consequence** if the party leaves the site unresolved.

The party does not need to clear the sites in a fixed order. The order changes which NPCs are available, which Sanity resources remain, and how aggressive Greaves becomes.

#### Site One — Isle of Reeds: The Grove of Bitter Palms

**Purpose:** Poison the living agricultural network of the Isle of Reeds so the land itself becomes a Soursight medium.

**Local Face:** Reed-walkers believe the Glimmer Bloom blight has entered its final stage. The water glows too brightly. Fish die with open mouths. Farmers on stilts hear children crying from below the flooded fields.

**Ritual Engine:** At the center of the grove, beneath the tallest bitter palm, the cult has sunk a mirror-lined root cage into the water. Inside it hangs the **Heart of the Blight**, fed by tainted Shinefruit pulp and blood drawn from captive Reed-walkers.

**Default Encounter Roster:**
- **Tender of the Bloom** if Senne remains loyal; otherwise replace with 1 **Shine-Whisperer** and 2 **Grave-Maw Zealots**.
- 2 **Glimmer Bloom Ambushers** hidden among the roots.
- 1 swarm of **Blight-fin Piranhas** in the flooded approach.

**Layer Reveals:**
- **Layer 1:** The water reflects open mouths instead of stars. Faint spiral sigils glow on submerged roots.
- **Layer 2:** The root cage becomes visible as a rib-like sphere beneath the water. Characters can target its three mirrored latches.
- **Layer 3:** The grove is revealed as a living diagram. Three root-lines connect the Heart to the Crags and Murk rituals. Severing the correct line weakens another site.

**Objectives:**
- Destroy or purify the Heart of the Blight.
- Rescue the Reed-walker captives before the cage floods completely.
- Convince Senne to defect, if she is present and has been seeded as redeemable.

**Mechanical Resolution:**
- The root cage has **AC 14, 45 HP**, immunity to poison and psychic damage, vulnerability to radiant damage, and three mirrored latches. Each latch can be opened with a **DC 15 Dexterity check using thieves' tools**, a **DC 16 Strength (Athletics)** check, or a **DC 15 Wisdom (Nature)** check made by a character who understands the Glimmer Bloom network.
- If all three latches are opened before the Heart is destroyed, the captives survive and the Heart's maximum HP is halved.
- A Tier 3 character can sever the root-line to one other ritual site with a **DC 17 Wisdom (Insight)** check. On a success, choose the Crags or Murk site; that site's primary ritual engine begins with 20 fewer HP or one disabled lair action.

**Failure Consequence:**
